Linear Programming: Dantzig and the Simplex Method JAH-PRAI-000001 · 1

Evidence: established Every historical fact is taken from the cited public sources; the demo is a labeled worked example of the record's own method, not a claim about a historical measurement.

Linear programming is the oldest prescriptive tool: given a goal to maximize or minimize and a set of linear constraints, find the best feasible choice. The simplex method, published by George Dantzig in 1947, solved it by walking from corner to corner of the feasible region, always improving, until no improvement was possible. Leonid Kantorovich had posed the problem in 1939 for the Soviet plywood industry, and the method became the workhorse of planning, logistics, and manufacturing. It works because the optimum of a linear problem always sits at a corner, so the search can be systematic rather than blind.

History

The history runs from Kantorovich's 1939 paper on the efficient use of resources, through Dantzig's 1947 simplex method, developed while he worked for the United States Air Force, to the post-war decades, when linear programming became the standard tool of operations research. The method spread through industry, airlines, and agriculture, wherever a scarce resource had to be allocated. The 1980s brought interior-point methods, which solve large problems faster, but the simplex method remains the standard teaching tool and the default for many problems, because it is transparent: it walks the corners, and every step can be checked.

Description

Linear programming is the oldest prescriptive tool: given a goal to maximize or minimize and a set of linear constraints, find the best feasible choice. The simplex method, published by George Dantzig in 1947, solved it by walking from corner to corner of the feasible region, always improving, until no improvement was possible. Leonid Kantorovich had posed the problem in 1939 for the Soviet plywood industry, and the method became the workhorse of planning, logistics, and manufacturing. It works because the optimum of a linear problem always sits at a corner, so the search can be systematic rather than blind.

The Problem

What is linear programming and the simplex method, from Kantorovich 1939 to Dantzig 1947, how does it work in practice, and what do its checkable figures show? This record answers that question with the dated history, the method, a worked demo whose figures are printed twice and hash-checked, and a plain statement of its limits.

The Worked Solution

Step 1: state the decision variables, the objective to maximize or minimize, and the constraints as linear inequalities. Step 2: find a feasible corner of the region the constraints define. Step 3: walk to a neighbouring corner that improves the objective, and repeat until no neighbour improves it. Step 4: report the optimum, the corner that achieves it, and the constraints that bind. The demo runs a small worked example of the choice step: it takes five production options with costs of 40, 55, 30, 70, and 50 and benefits of 120, 165, 95, 210, and 140, and reports the best option by benefit per cost, the total cost, and the total benefit. The demo is a worked example of the choice arithmetic, not a record of a historical optimization. The demo recomputes the checkable part of this solution from the key figures {"kantorovich_year": 1939, "dantzig_year": 1947, "best_ratio_worked": 3.1667, "total_cost_worked": 245, "total_benefit_worked": 730}.

The Answer

The checkable results are the dates: Kantorovich 1939, Dantzig 1947, and the method standard ever since. The worked example shows the choice arithmetic: the benefit-per-cost ratios are 3.0, 3.0, 3.1667, 3.0, and 2.8, so the best option is the third, with a ratio of 3.1667, and the totals are a cost of 245 and a benefit of 730. The result that matters is the method: the optimum is found by comparing ratios, and the comparison is transparent, so the decision can be explained and checked. The demo prints these checked figures: best: option 3, best_ratio: 3.1667, total_benefit: 730, total_cost: 245.

When It Applies

Linear programming applies wherever a goal and constraints can be stated linearly: production planning, logistics, diet design, and resource allocation. It applies where the optimum must be explained and the constraints must be listed, and only where the linearity assumption holds. It does not apply where the relationships are nonlinear, where the constraints cannot be written down, or where the optimum would be acted on without checking it against the real world.

Limits

The limits are the limits of linearity. Real problems are rarely linear: costs change with scale, benefits interact, and constraints are often not linear at all. The method also needs the problem to be stated completely, with every constraint written down, and a missing constraint will produce an optimum that is infeasible in practice. The honest account states the linearity assumption, lists the constraints, and checks the optimum against the real world before acting on it.

Math Proof

The mathematics is the mathematics of ratios. The five options have benefit-per-cost ratios of 120 over 40, which is 3.0; 165 over 55, which is 3.0; 95 over 30, which is 3.1667; 210 over 70, which is 3.0; and 140 over 50, which is 2.8. The best ratio is 3.1667, for the third option. The total cost is 40 plus 55 plus 30 plus 70 plus 50, which is 245, and the total benefit is 120 plus 165 plus 95 plus 210 plus 140, which is 730. These numbers describe one worked example, and they show the method: compare the ratios, pick the best, and report the totals. Formal statement: score = benefit / cost for each option; best = the option with the largest score; total cost = sum of costs; total benefit = sum of benefits. The equation states the choice check the demo performs: it scores every option by benefit divided by cost, picks the largest score as the best option, and totals the costs and benefits.

Science Proof

The science behind linear programming is the science of constrained optimization: a linear objective over a region defined by linear constraints always reaches its optimum at a corner of the region, so the search can walk the corners systematically. The simplex method exploits that geometry, moving from corner to corner along edges that improve the objective, and it stops when no edge improves it, which proves the corner is optimal. The science says the method works because the geometry guarantees it: a local optimum on a convex region is a global optimum, and the feasible region of a linear program is convex. Scientific relation: A prescriptive choice compares options by what each one returns for what it costs; the best option is the one with the largest benefit per unit of cost.. The demo recomputes the checkable arithmetic of this record from the stated figures, runs the computation twice, and compares the two SHA-256 digests; matching digests show the arithmetic is reproducible, and the method text states every input figure.

Working demo program

import hashlib
options = [('option 1', 40, 120), ('option 2', 55, 165), ('option 3', 30, 95), ('option 4', 70, 210), ('option 5', 50, 140)]
def compute():
    scored = [(b / c, name) for name, c, b in options]
    best_ratio_raw, best = max(scored)
    best_ratio = round(best_ratio_raw, 4)
    total_cost = sum(c for _, c, _ in options)
    total_benefit = sum(b for _, _, b in options)
    out = {"best": best, "best_ratio": best_ratio, "total_cost": total_cost, "total_benefit": total_benefit}
    return out
outputs = compute()
result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2 = compute()
result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs2.items()))
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
    print(f"{k}: {v}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

Full working program

import hashlib
class ProgJAHPRAI000001:
    """Working program for this record; run() recomputes the demo result."""
    def run(self):
        import hashlib
        options = [('option 1', 40, 120), ('option 2', 55, 165), ('option 3', 30, 95), ('option 4', 70, 210), ('option 5', 50, 140)]
        def compute():
            scored = [(b / c, name) for name, c, b in options]
            best_ratio_raw, best = max(scored)
            best_ratio = round(best_ratio_raw, 4)
            total_cost = sum(c for _, c, _ in options)
            total_benefit = sum(b for _, _, b in options)
            out = {"best": best, "best_ratio": best_ratio, "total_cost": total_cost, "total_benefit": total_benefit}
            return out
        outputs = compute()
        result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
        h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
        outputs2 = compute()
        result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs2.items()))
        h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
        for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
            print(f"{k}: {v}")
        print(f"Result: {result}")
        print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
        print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
        print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")
        return {"deterministic": h1 == h2, "result": result}
if __name__ == "__main__":
    print(ProgJAHPRAI000001().run())

Operations Research: The Wartime Science of Decisions JAH-PRAI-000002 · 2

Evidence: established Every historical fact is taken from the cited public sources; the demo is a labeled worked example of the record's own method, not a claim about a historical measurement.

Operations research is the science of making better decisions with mathematics: given a problem, model it, solve the model, and act on the solution. It was born in the Second World War, when Patrick Blackett's team, known as Blackett's Circus, used mathematics to improve radar, convoy routing, and bombing, and it became a discipline after the war, with a society founded in Britain in 1948 and the methods spreading to industry, transport, and government. It works because a decision problem, once modeled, can be solved systematically, and the solution can be tested before it is acted on.

History

The history runs from the wartime work of 1940, when Blackett's team applied operational analysis to anti-aircraft defence and convoy protection, through the post-war founding of the Operational Research Society in 1948 and the spread of the methods into industry, to the present, where operations research runs the logistics of the world. The wartime successes were concrete: better convoy sizes, better radar deployment, and better bombing patterns, all from mathematical analysis of operational data. The discipline's lesson is that a decision is a thing that can be studied, and that studying it with mathematics beats deciding by habit.

Description

Operations research is the science of making better decisions with mathematics: given a problem, model it, solve the model, and act on the solution. It was born in the Second World War, when Patrick Blackett's team, known as Blackett's Circus, used mathematics to improve radar, convoy routing, and bombing, and it became a discipline after the war, with a society founded in Britain in 1948 and the methods spreading to industry, transport, and government. It works because a decision problem, once modeled, can be solved systematically, and the solution can be tested before it is acted on.

The Problem

What is operations research, from Blackett's Circus in 1940 to the post-war discipline, how does it work in practice, and what do its checkable figures show? This record answers that question with the dated history, the method, a worked demo whose figures are printed twice and hash-checked, and a plain statement of its limits.

The Worked Solution

Step 1: state the decision problem, the objective, and the constraints. Step 2: build a model of the problem, with the variables, the objective, and the constraints written down. Step 3: solve the model, by optimization, simulation, or analysis. Step 4: test the solution against the real world, and act on it if it survives the test. The demo runs a small worked example of the counting step: it takes five wartime analyses with convoys studied of 18, 24, 12, 30, and 21, and reports the total, the mean, the smallest, and the largest. The demo is a worked example of the ledger arithmetic, not a record of a historical analysis. The demo recomputes the checkable part of this solution from the key figures {"blackett_year": 1940, "society_year": 1948, "total_convoys_worked": 105, "mean_convoys_worked": 21.0}.

The Answer

The checkable results are the dates: Blackett's Circus in 1940, and the Operational Research Society founded in 1948. The worked example shows the counting arithmetic: the five analyses study 18, 24, 12, 30, and 21 convoys, for a total of 105, a mean of 21.0, a smallest of 12, and a largest of 30. The result that matters is the method: a decision problem, once modeled, can be counted, solved, and tested, and the counting is the first step toward the solving. The demo prints these checked figures: largest: 30, mean: 21.0, n: 5, smallest: 12, total: 105.

When It Applies

Operations research applies wherever a decision can be modeled and the model can be tested: logistics, scheduling, inventory, and any operational problem with a clear objective. It applies where the assumptions are stated, the solution is tested, and a human decides. It does not apply where the problem cannot be stated, where the model cannot be tested, or where the solution would be acted on without checking it against the world.

Limits

The limits are the limits of modeling. A model leaves things out, and the things left out can matter more than the things included. A model fitted to the past may not fit the future, and a solution that is optimal for the model may be poor for the world. The honest account states the model's assumptions, tests the solution, and keeps a human in the loop, because the model is an aid to decision, not a substitute for it.

Math Proof

The mathematics is the mathematics of a ledger. The five analyses study 18, 24, 12, 30, and 21 convoys, which sum to 105, and 105 divided by 5 is 21.0. The smallest analysis studies 12 convoys and the largest studies 30. These numbers describe one worked example, and they make the discipline concrete: operations research counts, measures, and compares, and the counting is the foundation of the solving. Formal statement: total = sum of the counts; mean = total / n; smallest = min of the counts; largest = max of the counts. The equation states the ledger check the demo performs: it adds the named counts, divides by how many there are for the mean, and picks out the smallest and the largest.

Science Proof

The science behind operations research is the science of modeling decisions: a problem stated clearly can be solved clearly, and a problem stated vaguely can only be decided vaguely. The methods are optimization, queuing, simulation, and decision analysis, and each one fits a different kind of problem. The science says the model is the decision: the solution is only as good as the model, and the model must be tested against the world before the solution is acted on. The wartime work proved the point, because the models were tested in battle and the ones that worked were kept. Scientific relation: A ledger of counts is summarised by its total, its mean, and its extremes; those four numbers are the checkable summary of the record's figures..   • society_year: 1948

  • mean_convoys_worked: 21.0

Working demo program

import hashlib
ledger = [('analysis 1', 18), ('analysis 2', 24), ('analysis 3', 12), ('analysis 4', 30), ('analysis 5', 21)]
def compute():
    counts = [c for _, c in ledger]
    total = round(sum(counts), 4)
    mean = round(sum(counts) / len(counts), 4)
    smallest = min(counts)
    largest = max(counts)
    n = len(counts)
    out = {"total": total, "mean": mean, "smallest": smallest, "largest": largest, "n": n}
    return out
outputs = compute()
result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2 = compute()
result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs2.items()))
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
    print(f"{k}: {v}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

    def run(self):

            n = len(counts)
            out = {"total": total, "mean": mean, "smallest": smallest, "largest": largest, "n": n}
            return out

        h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
        outputs2 = compute()

Decision Theory: Expected Utility and the Rational Choice JAH-PRAI-000003 · 3

Evidence: established Every historical fact is taken from the cited public sources; the demo is a labeled worked example of the record's own method, not a claim about a historical measurement.

Decision theory is the mathematics of choosing well under uncertainty: given options with uncertain outcomes, choose the one with the highest expected utility, where utility measures how much each outcome is worth to the decision maker. The theory was founded by John von Neumann and Oskar Morgenstern in 1944, in their book on games and economic behaviour, and Leonard Savage extended it in 1954 to subjective probability, where the probabilities are the decision maker's own judgments. It works because it turns a hard choice into arithmetic: list the outcomes, weight them by their probabilities, and pick the largest total.

History

The history runs from von Neumann and Morgenstern's 1944 book, through Savage's 1954 foundations of statistics, to the present, where expected utility is the standard framework for decision analysis in business, medicine, and policy. The theory's power is its clarity: it states the axioms a rational choice must satisfy, and it shows that a choice satisfying them can be represented as maximizing expected utility. The challenges came from psychology, where Kahneman and Tversky showed that real people violate the axioms systematically, and the field's response was descriptive as well as normative: the theory says how a rational agent should choose, and the psychology says how people actually do.

Description

Decision theory is the mathematics of choosing well under uncertainty: given options with uncertain outcomes, choose the one with the highest expected utility, where utility measures how much each outcome is worth to the decision maker. The theory was founded by John von Neumann and Oskar Morgenstern in 1944, in their book on games and economic behaviour, and Leonard Savage extended it in 1954 to subjective probability, where the probabilities are the decision maker's own judgments. It works because it turns a hard choice into arithmetic: list the outcomes, weight them by their probabilities, and pick the largest total.

The Problem

What is decision theory, expected utility from von Neumann and Morgenstern 1944, and the rational choice, how does it work in practice, and what do its checkable figures show? This record answers that question with the dated history, the method, a worked demo whose figures are printed twice and hash-checked, and a plain statement of its limits.

The Worked Solution

Step 1: list the options and their possible outcomes. Step 2: assign a probability to each outcome and a utility to each outcome. Step 3: compute the expected utility of each option, the sum of each outcome's utility weighted by its probability. Step 4: choose the option with the highest expected utility, and report the calculation. The demo runs a small worked example of the choice step: it takes five options with costs of 100, 150, 80, 200, and 120 and benefits of 300, 420, 260, 560, and 340, and reports the best option by benefit per cost, the total cost, and the total benefit. The demo is a worked example of the choice arithmetic, not a record of a historical decision. The demo recomputes the checkable part of this solution from the key figures {"vnm_year": 1944, "savage_year": 1954, "best_ratio_worked": 3.25, "total_cost_worked": 650, "total_benefit_worked": 1880}.

The Answer

The checkable results are the dates: von Neumann and Morgenstern 1944, and Savage 1954. The worked example shows the choice arithmetic: the benefit-per-cost ratios are 3.0, 2.8, 3.25, 2.8, and 2.8333, so the best option is the third, with a ratio of 3.25, and the totals are a cost of 650 and a benefit of 1880. The result that matters is the method: a choice is justified by the calculation, and the calculation can be checked, argued with, and revised when the probabilities or the utilities change. The demo prints these checked figures: best: option 3, best_ratio: 3.25, total_benefit: 1880, total_cost: 650.

When It Applies

Decision theory applies wherever a choice under uncertainty must be justified: business, medicine, policy, and any decision where the options, the probabilities, and the values can be stated. It applies where the calculation is shown, the inputs are stated, and the sensitivity is reported. It does not apply where the probabilities are guesses treated as facts, where the utilities cannot be stated, or where the choice is made without showing the calculation.

Limits

The limits are the limits of the inputs. Expected utility needs probabilities and utilities, and both are often judgments rather than measurements. People violate the axioms, so the theory describes an ideal rather than a reality, and a decision that is rational by the theory can still be wrong if the inputs are wrong. The honest account states the probabilities and utilities, says where they came from, and reports how the choice changes when they change, because a decision analysis that hides its inputs is not an analysis at all.

Math Proof

The mathematics is the mathematics of ratios. The five options have benefit-per-cost ratios of 300 over 100, which is 3.0; 420 over 150, which is 2.8; 260 over 80, which is 3.25; 560 over 200, which is 2.8; and 340 over 120, which is 2.8333. The best ratio is 3.25, for the third option. The total cost is 100 plus 150 plus 80 plus 200 plus 120, which is 650, and the total benefit is 300 plus 420 plus 260 plus 560 plus 340, which is 1880. These numbers describe one worked example, and they show the method: compare the ratios, pick the best, and report the totals. Formal statement: score = benefit / cost for each option; best = the option with the largest score; total cost = sum of costs; total benefit = sum of benefits. The equation states the choice check the demo performs: it scores every option by benefit divided by cost, picks the largest score as the best option, and totals the costs and benefits.

Science Proof

The science behind decision theory is the science of rational choice under uncertainty: the axioms state what consistency requires, and the representation theorem shows that a consistent choice is a maximizing choice. The science says the theory is normative, not descriptive: it says how a rational agent should choose, and the empirical finding is that people often do not, which is a fact about people rather than a refutation of the theory. The method works where the probabilities and utilities can be stated honestly, and it fails where they are guessed and then treated as facts. Scientific relation: A prescriptive choice compares options by what each one returns for what it costs; the best option is the one with the largest benefit per unit of cost.. Working demo program

import hashlib
options = [('option 1', 100, 300), ('option 2', 150, 420), ('option 3', 80, 260), ('option 4', 200, 560), ('option 5', 120, 340)]
def compute():
    scored = [(b / c, name) for name, c, b in options]
    best_ratio_raw, best = max(scored)
    best_ratio = round(best_ratio_raw, 4)
    total_cost = sum(c for _, c, _ in options)
    total_benefit = sum(b for _, _, b in options)
    out = {"best": best, "best_ratio": best_ratio, "total_cost": total_cost, "total_benefit": total_benefit}
    return out
outputs = compute()
result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2 = compute()
result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs2.items()))
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
    print(f"{k}: {v}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

Markov Decision Processes: Bellman and the Optimal Policy JAH-PRAI-000004 · 4

Evidence: established Every historical fact is taken from the cited public sources; the demo is a labeled worked example of the record's own method, not a claim about a historical measurement.

A Markov decision process is the formal frame for prescribing actions over time: a system in a state, an action to take, a reward for taking it, and a transition to the next state, with the future discounted so that a reward now counts for more than a reward later. Richard Bellman founded the theory in 1957, with the optimality equation that states the value of a state as the best immediate reward plus the discounted value of the next state. The equation is the foundation of reinforcement learning, which learns the optimal policy by trying actions and observing rewards, and it is the standard frame for any problem where today's action shapes tomorrow's state.

History

The history runs from Bellman's 1957 book on dynamic programming, through the twentieth century, when the methods spread to control, operations, and economics, to the present, where reinforcement learning has made the frame the standard for sequential decision problems. The Bellman equation's power is its recursion: the value of a state depends on the value of the next state, so the optimal policy can be found by working backward from the future. The methods that followed, value iteration, policy iteration, and Q-learning, all solve the same equation in different ways, and the frame now underlies robotics, game playing, and resource management.

Description

A Markov decision process is the formal frame for prescribing actions over time: a system in a state, an action to take, a reward for taking it, and a transition to the next state, with the future discounted so that a reward now counts for more than a reward later. Richard Bellman founded the theory in 1957, with the optimality equation that states the value of a state as the best immediate reward plus the discounted value of the next state. The equation is the foundation of reinforcement learning, which learns the optimal policy by trying actions and observing rewards, and it is the standard frame for any problem where today's action shapes tomorrow's state.

The Problem

What is Markov decision processes and Bellman optimality, from 1957 to reinforcement learning, how does it work in practice, and what do its checkable figures show? This record answers that question with the dated history, the method, a worked demo whose figures are printed twice and hash-checked, and a plain statement of its limits.

The Worked Solution

Step 1: state the states, the actions, the rewards, and the transitions, with a discount factor for the future. Step 2: write the Bellman equation for the value of each state. Step 3: solve it by iteration, updating the values until they stop changing. Step 4: read the optimal policy from the values, choosing at each state the action with the highest value. The demo runs a small worked example of the choice step: it takes five actions with costs of 20, 35, 15, 45, and 25 and benefits of 90, 140, 70, 180, and 100, and reports the best action by benefit per cost, the total cost, and the total benefit. The demo is a worked example of the choice arithmetic, not a record of a historical control problem. The demo recomputes the checkable part of this solution from the key figures {"bellman_year": 1957, "best_ratio_worked": 4.6667, "total_cost_worked": 140, "total_benefit_worked": 580}.

The Answer

The checkable results are the dates: Bellman 1957, and the methods standard ever since. The worked example shows the choice arithmetic: the benefit-per-cost ratios are 4.5, 4.0, 4.6667, 4.0, and 4.0, so the best action is the third, with a ratio of 4.6667, and the totals are a cost of 140 and a benefit of 580. The result that matters is the method: the optimal action is the one with the best return per cost, and the Bellman equation generalizes that comparison across time, so the choice at each state accounts for the future it creates. The demo prints these checked figures: best: action 3, best_ratio: 4.6667, total_benefit: 580, total_cost: 140.

When It Applies

Markov decision processes apply wherever actions shape the future and the state can be stated: control, robotics, resource management, and game playing. They apply where the state captures what matters, the transitions are known or learnable, and the policy is tested before it is used. They do not apply where the state leaves out what matters, where the transitions cannot be learned, or where the discount is chosen without justification.

Limits

The limits are the limits of the frame. The state must capture everything that matters, and a state that leaves something out will produce a policy that is optimal for the model and poor for the world. The transitions and rewards must be known or learnable, and the discount factor is a judgment about the future that the mathematics cannot make. The honest account states the state, the transitions, and the discount, and tests the policy in simulation before acting on it.

Math Proof

The mathematics is the mathematics of ratios. The five actions have benefit-per-cost ratios of 90 over 20, which is 4.5; 140 over 35, which is 4.0; 70 over 15, which is 4.6667; 180 over 45, which is 4.0; and 100 over 25, which is 4.0. The best ratio is 4.6667, for the third action. The total cost is 20 plus 35 plus 15 plus 45 plus 25, which is 140, and the total benefit is 90 plus 140 plus 70 plus 180 plus 100, which is 580. These numbers describe one worked example, and they show the method: compare the ratios, pick the best, and report the totals. Formal statement: score = benefit / cost for each option; best = the option with the largest score; total cost = sum of costs; total benefit = sum of benefits. The equation states the choice check the demo performs: it scores every option by benefit divided by cost, picks the largest score as the best option, and totals the costs and benefits.

Science Proof

The science behind Markov decision processes is the science of sequential choice: today's action shapes tomorrow's state, so the value of an action includes the future it creates, discounted to the present. The Bellman equation states that value recursively, and the recursion is what makes the problem solvable: the optimal policy can be found by working backward from the future, or by iterating until the values settle. The science says the frame works when the state captures what matters, the transitions are known or learnable, and the discount reflects the real preference for now over later. Scientific relation: A prescriptive choice compares options by what each one returns for what it costs; the best option is the one with the largest benefit per unit of cost.. Skeptical analysis

The limits are the limits of the frame. The state must capture everything that matters, and a state that leaves something out will produce a policy that is optimal for the model and poor for the world. The transitions and rewards must be known or learnable, and the discount factor is a judgment about the future that the mathematics cannot make. The honest account states the state, the transitions, and the discount, and tests the policy in simulation before acting on it.

Working demo program

import hashlib
options = [('action 1', 20, 90), ('action 2', 35, 140), ('action 3', 15, 70), ('action 4', 45, 180), ('action 5', 25, 100)]
def compute():
    scored = [(b / c, name) for name, c, b in options]
    best_ratio_raw, best = max(scored)
    best_ratio = round(best_ratio_raw, 4)
    total_cost = sum(c for _, c, _ in options)
    total_benefit = sum(b for _, _, b in options)
    out = {"best": best, "best_ratio": best_ratio, "total_cost": total_cost, "total_benefit": total_benefit}
    return out
outputs = compute()
result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2 = compute()
result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs2.items()))
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
    print(f"{k}: {v}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

Recommender Systems as Prescription: From GroupLens to Netflix JAH-PRAI-000005 · 5

Evidence: established Every historical fact is taken from the cited public sources; the demo is a labeled worked example of the record's own method, not a claim about a historical measurement.

A recommender system is prescription at the scale of millions: given what a person has liked, prescribe what they might like next. Collaborative filtering, first demonstrated in the GroupLens system in 1994, finds people with similar tastes and recommends what they liked. The Netflix Prize, run from 2006 to 2009 with a million-dollar prize, showed how far the methods could go, as teams cut the prediction error by ten percent and the winning methods combined dozens of models. The systems now shape what people watch, buy, and read, which makes their prescription a matter of public concern as well as of commerce.

History

The history runs from GroupLens in 1994, the first collaborative-filtering system for news, through the spread of recommenders in e-commerce in the late 1990s, to the Netflix Prize of 2006 to 2009, which drew thousands of teams and advanced the state of the art. The prize's lesson was that ensembles win: the winning team combined many models, and the gain came from diversity rather than from any single method. The systems spread to music, video, shopping, and news, and the concerns spread with them: a recommender that optimizes engagement can narrow what people see, and a system that prescribes must be judged by what it prescribes, not only by how accurately it predicts.

Description

A recommender system is prescription at the scale of millions: given what a person has liked, prescribe what they might like next. Collaborative filtering, first demonstrated in the GroupLens system in 1994, finds people with similar tastes and recommends what they liked. The Netflix Prize, run from 2006 to 2009 with a million-dollar prize, showed how far the methods could go, as teams cut the prediction error by ten percent and the winning methods combined dozens of models. The systems now shape what people watch, buy, and read, which makes their prescription a matter of public concern as well as of commerce.

The Problem

What is recommender systems as prescriptive AI, from collaborative filtering to the Netflix Prize, how does it work in practice, and what do its checkable figures show? This record answers that question with the dated history, the method, a worked demo whose figures are printed twice and hash-checked, and a plain statement of its limits.

The Worked Solution

Step 1: take each user's history as ratings or interactions. Step 2: find similar users or similar items, and predict the rating for the unseen items. Step 3: rank the items by predicted rating, and prescribe the top ones. Step 4: measure the outcome, whether the user engaged, and update the model. The demo runs a small worked example of the measurement step: it takes five predicted ratings, 4.5, 3.0, 5.0, 2.5, and 4.0, against actual ratings of 4.0, 3.5, 4.5, 3.0, and 4.0, and reports the mean absolute error and the root mean square error. The demo is a worked example of the error arithmetic, not a record of a historical recommender. The demo recomputes the checkable part of this solution from the key figures {"grouplens_year": 1994, "netflix_prize_start": 2006, "mae_worked": 0.4, "rmse_worked": 0.4472}.

The Answer

The checkable results are the dates: GroupLens 1994, and the Netflix Prize 2006 to 2009, with a million-dollar prize and a ten percent improvement target. The worked example shows the error arithmetic: the gaps are 0.5, 0.5, 0.5, 0.5, and 0.0, giving a mean absolute error of 0.4 and a root mean square error of 0.4472, with a largest gap of 0.5. The result that matters is the scale: a mean error of four tenths of a star on a five-star scale is the level the Netflix Prize teams worked at, and the ten percent improvement they achieved was measured against a baseline that was already good. The demo prints these checked figures: mae: 0.4, max_err: 0.5, n: 5, rmse: 0.4472.

When It Applies

Recommender systems apply wherever a large catalogue must be narrowed to a few suggestions: video, music, shopping, and news. They apply where the accuracy is measured, the diversity is measured, and the user can control the prescription. They do not apply where the prescription would narrow what people see without their consent, where the popular would crowd out the new, or where the accuracy is reported without the diversity.

Limits

The limits are the limits of prescribing taste. A recommender can narrow what people see, creating a filter bubble, and it can amplify what is already popular, because popular items have the most data. It cannot know what a person would like if they tried it, only what people like them liked, and it will reproduce the biases in the history it was trained on. The honest account measures the diversity of what is prescribed alongside the accuracy, and it gives the user control over the prescription, because a prescription without consent is a manipulation.

Math Proof

The mathematics is the mathematics of error measurement. The five gaps are 0.5, 0.5, 0.5, 0.5, and 0.0, which sum to 2.0, so the mean absolute error is 2.0 divided by 5, which is 0.4. The squared gaps are 0.25, 0.25, 0.25, 0.25, and 0.0, which sum to 1.0, so the mean squared error is 0.2 and the root mean square error is the square root of 0.2, which is 0.4472. The largest single gap is 0.5. Small errors on a worked example prove the arithmetic, not the recommender; the recommender was proved, or not, by its held-out error and by what its users did with its prescriptions. Formal statement: MAE = (1/n) * sum |pred_i - actual_i|; RMSE = sqrt((1/n) * sum (pred_i - actual_i)^2); max error = max |pred_i - actual_i|. The equation states the forecast-error check the demo performs: it takes the worked example's predicted and actual pairs, averages the absolute errors for the MAE, and takes the square root of the averaged squared errors for the RMSE.

Science Proof

The science behind recommenders is the science of similarity: people who agreed in the past will agree in the future, and items that were liked together will be liked together again. The science says the signal is real but thin, because most users rate few items, so the matrix is sparse, and the methods must fill the gaps. The field's lesson is that the ensemble wins, because different models capture different parts of the signal, and the combination is more robust than any single model. The concern is the prescription: a system that optimizes engagement will prescribe what keeps people watching, which is not always what is good for them. Scientific relation: A forecast is judged by the gap between what was predicted and what happened; the MAE averages that gap and the RMSE punishes large gaps more heavily.. The demo recomputes the checkable arithmetic of this record from the stated figures, runs the computation twice, and compares the two SHA-256 digests; matching digests show the arithmetic is reproducible, and the method text states every input figure.

Working demo program

import hashlib
pairs = [(4.5, 4.0), (3.0, 3.5), (5.0, 4.5), (2.5, 3.0), (4.0, 4.0)]
def compute():
    errs = [abs(p - a) for p, a in pairs]
    mae = round(sum(errs) / len(errs), 4)
    rmse = round((sum(e * e for e in errs) / len(errs)) ** 0.5, 4)
    max_err = round(max(errs), 4)
    n = len(pairs)
    out = {"mae": mae, "rmse": rmse, "max_err": max_err, "n": n}
    return out
outputs = compute()
result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2 = compute()
result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs2.items()))
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
    print(f"{k}: {v}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

Logistics Prescription: Routing and the Vehicle Routing Problem JAH-PRAI-000006 · 6

Evidence: established Every historical fact is taken from the cited public sources; the demo is a labeled worked example of the record's own method, not a claim about a historical measurement.

Logistics is prescription at the scale of fleets: given deliveries to make, vehicles to make them with, and roads to drive on, prescribe the routes that cost least. The vehicle routing problem, posed by Dantzig and Ramser in 1959, states it formally: a fleet must visit a set of customers, each vehicle has a capacity, and the goal is to minimize the total distance or cost. The problem is hard, in the formal sense that no fast exact method exists for large instances, so the practice is heuristics and metaheuristics that find good routes quickly. The systems now route the world's deliveries, and the savings are counted in fuel, time, and vehicles.

History

The history runs from Dantzig and Ramser's 1959 paper, through the decades of heuristic development, to the present, where routing systems handle thousands of stops and update in real time as traffic changes. The methods are local search, tabu search, and genetic algorithms, all of which improve a route by small changes, and the practice is to solve, test, and re-solve as the day unfolds. The economics drive it: a route that is a few percent shorter, multiplied by a fleet and a year, is a large saving, and the systems that find those percents are worth their cost.

Description

Logistics is prescription at the scale of fleets: given deliveries to make, vehicles to make them with, and roads to drive on, prescribe the routes that cost least. The vehicle routing problem, posed by Dantzig and Ramser in 1959, states it formally: a fleet must visit a set of customers, each vehicle has a capacity, and the goal is to minimize the total distance or cost. The problem is hard, in the formal sense that no fast exact method exists for large instances, so the practice is heuristics and metaheuristics that find good routes quickly. The systems now route the world's deliveries, and the savings are counted in fuel, time, and vehicles.

The Problem

What is prescriptive analytics in logistics, routing, and the vehicle routing problem from 1959, how does it work in practice, and what do its checkable figures show? This record answers that question with the dated history, the method, a worked demo whose figures are printed twice and hash-checked, and a plain statement of its limits.

The Worked Solution

Step 1: take the deliveries as stops with demands and time windows. Step 2: build an initial set of routes, one per vehicle, respecting capacity. Step 3: improve the routes by local moves, swapping stops between routes and reordering within them, until no move improves the total. Step 4: report the routes, the total distance, and the savings against the baseline. The demo runs a small worked example of the choice step: it takes five route options with costs of 120, 95, 150, 80, and 110 and benefits of 300, 260, 380, 220, and 290, and reports the best option by benefit per cost, the total cost, and the total benefit. The demo is a worked example of the choice arithmetic, not a record of a historical routing. The demo recomputes the checkable part of this solution from the key figures {"vrp_year": 1959, "best_ratio_worked": 2.75, "total_cost_worked": 555, "total_benefit_worked": 1450}.

The Answer

The checkable results are the dates: Dantzig and Ramser posed the vehicle routing problem in 1959, and routing systems are standard today. The worked example shows the choice arithmetic: the benefit-per-cost ratios are 2.5, 2.7368, 2.5333, 2.75, and 2.6364, so the best option is the fourth, with a ratio of 2.75, and the totals are a cost of 555 and a benefit of 1450. The result that matters is the method: the best route is the one that returns the most per unit of cost, and the heuristic search finds it by improving a good route rather than by enumerating all of them. The demo prints these checked figures: best: route 4, best_ratio: 2.75, total_benefit: 1450, total_cost: 555.

When It Applies

Logistics prescription applies wherever a fleet must visit many stops under capacity and time constraints: delivery, collection, and service routing. It applies where the heuristic is tested, the routes are updated as conditions change, and the savings are measured against the baseline. It does not apply where the optimum is required and the problem is large, where the data is stale, or where a heuristic route is presented as if it were optimal.

Limits

The limits are the limits of heuristics. A heuristic finds a good route, not the best, and the gap between good and best is unknown on large problems. The routes also depend on the data: traffic, road closures, and customer availability change during the day, so a route planned in the morning may be poor by the afternoon. The honest account reports the gap where it can be measured, updates the routes as the day unfolds, and never presents a heuristic route as if it were optimal.

Math Proof

The mathematics is the mathematics of ratios. The five options have benefit-per-cost ratios of 300 over 120, which is 2.5; 260 over 95, which is 2.7368; 380 over 150, which is 2.5333; 220 over 80, which is 2.75; and 290 over 110, which is 2.6364. The best ratio is 2.75, for the fourth option. The total cost is 120 plus 95 plus 150 plus 80 plus 110, which is 555, and the total benefit is 300 plus 260 plus 380 plus 220 plus 290, which is 1450. These numbers describe one worked example, and they show the method: compare the ratios, pick the best, and report the totals. Formal statement: score = benefit / cost for each option; best = the option with the largest score; total cost = sum of costs; total benefit = sum of benefits. The equation states the choice check the demo performs: it scores every option by benefit divided by cost, picks the largest score as the best option, and totals the costs and benefits.

Science Proof

The science behind routing is the science of combinatorial optimization: the number of possible routes grows faster than any computer can enumerate, so the exact optimum is out of reach for large problems, and the practical answer is a heuristic that finds a good route quickly. The science says the heuristic must be tested against the optimum on small problems, where the optimum can be computed, and against the baseline on large ones, where it cannot. The method works because a good route is usually close to the optimum, and the savings from a good route are most of the savings available. Scientific relation: A prescriptive choice compares options by what each one returns for what it costs; the best option is the one with the largest benefit per unit of cost.. History

The history runs from Dantzig and Ramser's 1959 paper, through the decades of heuristic development, to the present, where routing systems handle thousands of stops and update in real time as traffic changes. The methods are local search, tabu search, and genetic algorithms, all of which improve a route by small changes, and the practice is to solve, test, and re-solve as the day unfolds. The economics drive it: a route that is a few percent shorter, multiplied by a fleet and a year, is a large saving, and the systems that find those percents are worth their cost.

Problem

What is prescriptive analytics in logistics, routing, and the vehicle routing problem from 1959, how does it work in practice, and what do its checkable figures show? This record answers that question with the dated history, the method, a worked demo whose figures are printed twice and hash-checked, and a plain statement of its limits.

Method

Step 1: take the deliveries as stops with demands and time windows. Step 2: build an initial set of routes, one per vehicle, respecting capacity. Step 3: improve the routes by local moves, swapping stops between routes and reordering within them, until no move improves the total. Step 4: report the routes, the total distance, and the savings against the baseline. The demo runs a small worked example of the choice step: it takes five route options with costs of 120, 95, 150, 80, and 110 and benefits of 300, 260, 380, 220, and 290, and reports the best option by benefit per cost, the total cost, and the total benefit. The demo is a worked example of the choice arithmetic, not a record of a historical routing.

Formal equation, in plain words

The equation states the choice check the demo performs: it scores every option by benefit divided by cost, picks the largest score as the best option, and totals the costs and benefits.

Scientific equation

A prescriptive choice compares options by what each one returns for what it costs; the best option is the one with the largest benefit per unit of cost.

Results reported

The checkable results are the dates: Dantzig and Ramser posed the vehicle routing problem in 1959, and routing systems are standard today. The worked example shows the choice arithmetic: the benefit-per-cost ratios are 2.5, 2.7368, 2.5333, 2.75, and 2.6364, so the best option is the fourth, with a ratio of 2.75, and the totals are a cost of 555 and a benefit of 1450. The result that matters is the method: the best route is the one that returns the most per unit of cost, and the heuristic search finds it by improving a good route rather than by enumerating all of them. The demo prints these checked figures: best: route 4, best_ratio: 2.75, total_benefit: 1450, total_cost: 555.

Skeptical analysis

The limits are the limits of heuristics. A heuristic finds a good route, not the best, and the gap between good and best is unknown on large problems. The routes also depend on the data: traffic, road closures, and customer availability change during the day, so a route planned in the morning may be poor by the afternoon. The honest account reports the gap where it can be measured, updates the routes as the day unfolds, and never presents a heuristic route as if it were optimal.

Working demo program

import hashlib
options = [('route 1', 120, 300), ('route 2', 95, 260), ('route 3', 150, 380), ('route 4', 80, 220), ('route 5', 110, 290)]
def compute():
    scored = [(b / c, name) for name, c, b in options]
    best_ratio_raw, best = max(scored)
    best_ratio = round(best_ratio_raw, 4)
    total_cost = sum(c for _, c, _ in options)
    total_benefit = sum(b for _, _, b in options)
    out = {"best": best, "best_ratio": best_ratio, "total_cost": total_cost, "total_benefit": total_benefit}
    return out
outputs = compute()
result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2 = compute()
result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs2.items()))
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
    print(f"{k}: {v}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

Healthcare Prescription: Treatment Optimization JAH-PRAI-000007 · 7

Evidence: established Every historical fact is taken from the cited public sources; the demo is a labeled worked example of the record's own method, not a claim about a historical measurement.

Healthcare prescription is the most consequential form of the field: given a patient, prescribe the treatment that gives the best outcome, not merely predict the outcome of doing nothing. The methods are decision analysis, Markov models of disease progression, and optimization over treatment sequences, and the data is the patient's history and the evidence from trials. The stakes are the reason the field is careful: a prescription that is wrong harms a person, so the evidence must be strong, the uncertainty must be stated, and the clinician must decide. The systems that survive are the ones that advise, explain, and defer.

History

The history runs from the decision analysis of the 1970s, through the Markov models of disease of the 1980s and 1990s, to the present, where treatment optimization runs in oncology, diabetes, and chronic disease management. The methods model the disease as a process with states, the treatments as actions, and the outcomes as rewards, which is the Markov decision frame applied to medicine. The practice is now standard in guideline development, where the pathways are optimized before they are published, and in personalised medicine, where the prescription is tailored to the patient. The discipline is the evidence: a prescription must be supported by trials, and the uncertainty must be reported.

Description

Healthcare prescription is the most consequential form of the field: given a patient, prescribe the treatment that gives the best outcome, not merely predict the outcome of doing nothing. The methods are decision analysis, Markov models of disease progression, and optimization over treatment sequences, and the data is the patient's history and the evidence from trials. The stakes are the reason the field is careful: a prescription that is wrong harms a person, so the evidence must be strong, the uncertainty must be stated, and the clinician must decide. The systems that survive are the ones that advise, explain, and defer.

The Problem

What is prescriptive analytics in healthcare, treatment optimization, and clinical pathways, how does it work in practice, and what do its checkable figures show? This record answers that question with the dated history, the method, a worked demo whose figures are printed twice and hash-checked, and a plain statement of its limits.

The Worked Solution

Step 1: take the patient's state as input, diagnosis, history, and measurements. Step 2: model the disease as a process with states and transitions, and the treatments as actions with outcomes. Step 3: optimize the treatment sequence, maximizing the expected outcome under the uncertainty. Step 4: report the prescription with its evidence, its uncertainty, and the alternatives, and let the clinician decide. The demo runs a small worked example of the measurement step: it takes five treatment options with predicted outcome scores of 0.85, 0.72, 0.9, 0.65, and 0.8 against observed scores of 0.82, 0.75, 0.88, 0.68, and 0.78, and reports the mean absolute error and the root mean square error. The demo is a worked example of the error arithmetic, not a record of a historical treatment. The demo recomputes the checkable part of this solution from the key figures {"mae_worked": 0.026, "rmse_worked": 0.0265, "max_gap_worked": 0.03}.

The Answer

The checkable results are the practice and the dates: decision analysis from the 1970s, Markov disease models from the 1980s and 1990s, and treatment optimization standard today. The worked example shows the error arithmetic: the gaps are 0.03, 0.03, 0.02, 0.03, and 0.02, giving a mean absolute error of 0.026 and a root mean square error of 0.0265, with a largest gap of 0.03. The result that matters is the size: a mean error of 0.026 on outcome scores is small, but the decision is still the clinician's, because the scores are predictions and the patient is a person. The demo prints these checked figures: mae: 0.026, max_err: 0.03, n: 5, rmse: 0.0265.

When It Applies

Healthcare prescription applies wherever a treatment sequence must be chosen and the evidence is strong: oncology, diabetes, and chronic disease management. It applies where the model is validated, the uncertainty is reported, and the clinician decides. It does not apply where the evidence is thin, where the patient differs from the trial population, or where the prescription would be acted on without a clinician checking it.

Limits

The limits are the limits of prescribing for a person. The evidence is about populations, and the patient is an individual, so the prescription must be tailored and the tailoring must be justified. The model cannot see what was never measured, and a treatment that is optimal on average may be wrong for this patient. The honest account reports the evidence, the uncertainty, and the alternatives, and keeps the clinician in the loop, because the prescription is advice, not a command.

Math Proof

The mathematics is the mathematics of error measurement. The five gaps are 0.03, 0.03, 0.02, 0.03, and 0.02, which sum to 0.13, so the mean absolute error is 0.13 divided by 5, which is 0.026. The squared gaps are 0.0009, 0.0009, 0.0004, 0.0009, and 0.0004, which sum to 0.0035, so the mean squared error is 0.0007 and the root mean square error is the square root of 0.0007, which is 0.0265. The largest single gap is 0.03. Small errors on a worked example prove the arithmetic, not the treatment; the treatment was proved, or not, by its trials. Formal statement: MAE = (1/n) * sum |pred_i - actual_i|; RMSE = sqrt((1/n) * sum (pred_i - actual_i)^2); max error = max |pred_i - actual_i|. The equation states the forecast-error check the demo performs: it takes the worked example's predicted and actual pairs, averages the absolute errors for the MAE, and takes the square root of the averaged squared errors for the RMSE.

Science Proof

The science behind treatment optimization is the science of evidence-based decision: a treatment's value is measured by trials, the disease's course is modeled as a process, and the prescription is the sequence that maximizes the expected outcome. The science says the model must be validated on the population it will serve, because a treatment that works on average may not work for this patient, and the uncertainty must be reported, because a prescription without its uncertainty is a guess presented as a fact. The method works where the evidence is strong and the model is validated, and it fails where the evidence is thin or the patient differs from the trial population. Scientific relation: A forecast is judged by the gap between what was predicted and what happened; the MAE averages that gap and the RMSE punishes large gaps more heavily.. Working demo program

import hashlib
pairs = [(0.85, 0.82), (0.72, 0.75), (0.9, 0.88), (0.65, 0.68), (0.8, 0.78)]
def compute():
    errs = [abs(p - a) for p, a in pairs]
    mae = round(sum(errs) / len(errs), 4)
    rmse = round((sum(e * e for e in errs) / len(errs)) ** 0.5, 4)
    max_err = round(max(errs), 4)
    n = len(pairs)
    out = {"mae": mae, "rmse": rmse, "max_err": max_err, "n": n}
    return out
outputs = compute()
result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2 = compute()
result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs2.items()))
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
    print(f"{k}: {v}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

Energy Prescription: Grid Optimization and Unit Commitment JAH-PRAI-000008 · 8

Evidence: established Every historical fact is taken from the cited public sources; the demo is a labeled worked example of the record's own method, not a claim about a historical measurement.

Energy prescription is optimization where the failure is a blackout: given the demand, the generators, and the transmission lines, prescribe which generators to run, when to start them, and how much power each should produce. Unit commitment, the decision of which generators to switch on, and economic dispatch, the decision of how much each should produce, are solved every day by grid operators, and the savings are counted in fuel and in reliability. The methods are mixed-integer programming and optimization under uncertainty, because the demand is uncertain, the wind and sun are uncertain, and the grid must balance every second.

History

The history runs from the early economic dispatch of the mid-twentieth century, through the unit-commitment models of the 1970s and 1980s, to the present, where the optimization runs daily and the uncertainty from wind and solar has made it harder and more important. The grid's physics drives it: supply must equal demand at every instant, a generator takes hours to start, and a wrong commitment is expensive or dangerous. The practice is now standard in every large grid, and the methods are the backbone of the energy transition, because a grid with much wind and solar needs optimization more than a grid without it.

Description

Energy prescription is optimization where the failure is a blackout: given the demand, the generators, and the transmission lines, prescribe which generators to run, when to start them, and how much power each should produce. Unit commitment, the decision of which generators to switch on, and economic dispatch, the decision of how much each should produce, are solved every day by grid operators, and the savings are counted in fuel and in reliability. The methods are mixed-integer programming and optimization under uncertainty, because the demand is uncertain, the wind and sun are uncertain, and the grid must balance every second.

The Problem

What is prescriptive analytics in energy, grid optimization, unit commitment, and demand response, how does it work in practice, and what do its checkable figures show? This record answers that question with the dated history, the method, a worked demo whose figures are printed twice and hash-checked, and a plain statement of its limits.

The Worked Solution

Step 1: take the demand forecast and the generator data as input, capacities, costs, and start times. Step 2: solve the unit commitment, choosing which generators to run and when to start them. Step 3: solve the economic dispatch, choosing how much each running generator should produce. Step 4: report the schedule, the cost, and the reserve, and re-solve as the forecast updates. The demo runs a small worked example of the choice step: it takes five generator options with costs of 60, 45, 80, 55, and 70 and benefits of 200, 160, 260, 190, and 230, and reports the best option by benefit per cost, the total cost, and the total benefit. The demo is a worked example of the choice arithmetic, not a record of a historical dispatch. The demo recomputes the checkable part of this solution from the key figures {"best_ratio_worked": 3.5556, "total_cost_worked": 310, "total_benefit_worked": 1040}.

The Answer

When It Applies

Energy prescription applies wherever a grid must be balanced and the generators must be scheduled: unit commitment, economic dispatch, and demand response. It applies where the optimization is re-solved as the forecast updates, the reserve is kept, and the schedule is checked against the grid's limits. It does not apply where the forecast is treated as certain, where the reserve is cut to save money, or where the schedule would be run without checking it against the transmission limits.

Limits

The limits are the limits of forecasting the grid. The demand forecast is uncertain, the wind and solar output is uncertain, and a schedule that is optimal for the forecast may be poor for the reality. The optimization also depends on the model of the grid, and a model that misstates a transmission limit will produce a schedule that cannot be run. The honest account re-solves as the forecast updates, keeps a reserve, and never presents a schedule as if the future were certain.

Math Proof

The mathematics is the mathematics of ratios. The five options have benefit-per-cost ratios of 200 over 60, which is 3.3333; 160 over 45, which is 3.5556; 260 over 80, which is 3.25; 190 over 55, which is 3.4545; and 230 over 70, which is 3.2857. These numbers describe one worked example, and they show the method: compare the ratios, pick the best, and report the totals. Formal statement: score = benefit / cost for each option; best = the option with the largest score; total cost = sum of costs; total benefit = sum of benefits. The equation states the choice check the demo performs: it scores every option by benefit divided by cost, picks the largest score as the best option, and totals the costs and benefits.

Science Proof

The science behind grid optimization is the science of balancing under uncertainty: the grid must match supply to demand at every instant, the generators have different costs and different speeds, and the demand and the renewable output are uncertain. The optimization solves the commitment and the dispatch together, so the schedule is feasible and cheap, and the reserve is the insurance against the uncertainty. The science says the problem is hard because it is mixed-integer, with on-off decisions as well as continuous ones, and the methods that solve it are the best the field has. Scientific relation: A prescriptive choice compares options by what each one returns for what it costs; the best option is the one with the largest benefit per unit of cost.. History

The history runs from the early economic dispatch of the mid-twentieth century, through the unit-commitment models of the 1970s and 1980s, to the present, where the optimization runs daily and the uncertainty from wind and solar has made it harder and more important. The grid's physics drives it: supply must equal demand at every instant, a generator takes hours to start, and a wrong commitment is expensive or dangerous. The practice is now standard in every large grid, and the methods are the backbone of the energy transition, because a grid with much wind and solar needs optimization more than a grid without it.

Working demo program

import hashlib
options = [('generator 1', 60, 200), ('generator 2', 45, 160), ('generator 3', 80, 260), ('generator 4', 55, 190), ('generator 5', 70, 230)]
def compute():
    scored = [(b / c, name) for name, c, b in options]
    best_ratio_raw, best = max(scored)
    best_ratio = round(best_ratio_raw, 4)
    total_cost = sum(c for _, c, _ in options)
    total_benefit = sum(b for _, _, b in options)
    out = {"best": best, "best_ratio": best_ratio, "total_cost": total_cost, "total_benefit": total_benefit}
    return out
outputs = compute()
result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2 = compute()
result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs2.items()))
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
    print(f"{k}: {v}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

Simulation-Based Prescription: Monte Carlo and Digital Twins JAH-PRAI-000009 · 9

Evidence: established Every historical fact is taken from the cited public sources; the demo is a labeled worked example of the record's own method, not a claim about a historical measurement.

Simulation-based prescription is decision by rehearsal: build a model of the system, run it many times with different random inputs, and prescribe the action that does best across the runs. The Monte Carlo method, developed in the 1940s by Stanislaw Ulam, John von Neumann, and Nicholas Metropolis, made the rehearsal possible by using random sampling to explore what the mathematics could not solve. The digital twin is the modern form: a live simulation of a factory, a grid, or a city, updated with real data, in which decisions can be tested before they are taken. The method works where the system is too complex to optimize directly, and the simulation is the only way to see the consequences.

History

The history runs from the Monte Carlo work of the 1940s, at Los Alamos, where Ulam, von Neumann, and Metropolis used random sampling to solve problems in neutron diffusion, through the post-war spread of simulation into industry and operations, to the present, where digital twins run alongside the systems they mirror. The method's power is its generality: any system that can be simulated can be rehearsed, and the rehearsal can cover the cases the mathematics cannot. The practice is now standard in manufacturing, energy, and logistics, where the twin is used to test a change before the change is made.

Description

Simulation-based prescription is decision by rehearsal: build a model of the system, run it many times with different random inputs, and prescribe the action that does best across the runs. The Monte Carlo method, developed in the 1940s by Stanislaw Ulam, John von Neumann, and Nicholas Metropolis, made the rehearsal possible by using random sampling to explore what the mathematics could not solve. The digital twin is the modern form: a live simulation of a factory, a grid, or a city, updated with real data, in which decisions can be tested before they are taken. The method works where the system is too complex to optimize directly, and the simulation is the only way to see the consequences.

The Problem

What is simulation-based prescription, Monte Carlo methods, and digital twins for decision, how does it work in practice, and what do its checkable figures show? This record answers that question with the dated history, the method, a worked demo whose figures are printed twice and hash-checked, and a plain statement of its limits.

The Worked Solution

Step 1: build a simulation of the system, with its rules, its randomness, and its inputs. Step 2: run the simulation many times for each candidate action, sampling the random inputs afresh each time. Step 3: compare the actions by their average outcome across the runs, and by their worst case. Step 4: prescribe the action that does best on average without doing badly in the worst case, and report the range. The demo runs a small worked example of the counting step: it takes five simulation batches with runs completed of 1000, 1200, 900, 1500, and 1100, and reports the total, the mean, the smallest, and the largest. The demo is a worked example of the ledger arithmetic, not a record of a historical simulation. The demo recomputes the checkable part of this solution from the key figures {"monte_carlo_decade": 1940, "total_runs_worked": 5700, "mean_runs_worked": 1140.0}.

The Answer

The checkable results are the dates: Monte Carlo developed in the 1940s, and digital twins standard in the 2010s and 2020s. The worked example shows the counting arithmetic: the five batches complete 1000, 1200, 900, 1500, and 1100 runs, for a total of 5700, a mean of 1140.0, a smallest of 900, and a largest of 1500. The result that matters is the scale: thousands of runs are needed to see the range of outcomes, and the mean alone is not enough, because the worst case is where the risk lives. The demo prints these checked figures: largest: 1500, mean: 1140.0, n: 5, smallest: 900, total: 5700.

When It Applies

Simulation-based prescription applies wherever a system is too complex to optimize directly and can be modeled faithfully: manufacturing, energy, logistics, and any decision where the consequences can be rehearsed. It applies where the simulation is validated, the worst case is reported, and the runs are numerous enough to be stable. It does not apply where the model is unvalidated, where the worst case is hidden, or where a simulated outcome is presented as if it were measured.

Limits

The limits are the limits of the model. A simulation prescribes well only where its model is faithful, and a model that leaves out a failure mode will prescribe an action that fails in exactly that mode. The runs are also expensive, and the number needed grows with the rarity of the events that matter. The honest account validates the simulation against the real system, reports the worst case alongside the average, and never presents a simulated outcome as if it were a measured one.

Math Proof

The mathematics is the mathematics of a ledger. The five batches complete 1000, 1200, 900, 1500, and 1100 runs, which sum to 5700, and 5700 divided by 5 is 1140.0. The smallest batch completes 900 runs and the largest completes 1500. These numbers describe one worked example, and they make the method concrete: simulation is counting, and the count must be large enough that the average is stable and the worst case has been seen. Formal statement: total = sum of the counts; mean = total / n; smallest = min of the counts; largest = max of the counts. The equation states the ledger check the demo performs: it adds the named counts, divides by how many there are for the mean, and picks out the smallest and the largest.

Science Proof

The science behind simulation is the science of the law of large numbers: the average of many random runs converges to the expected outcome, and the spread of the runs shows the risk. The method works where the simulation is faithful, because the rehearsal is only as good as the model, and it fails where the model leaves out what matters. The digital twin adds the live data, so the simulation tracks the real system, and the prescription can be tested against the present state rather than a generic one. The science says the twin is a model, not a copy, and the gap between the two is the risk. Scientific relation: A ledger of counts is summarised by its total, its mean, and its extremes; those four numbers are the checkable summary of the record's figures.. Working demo program

import hashlib
ledger = [('batch 1', 1000), ('batch 2', 1200), ('batch 3', 900), ('batch 4', 1500), ('batch 5', 1100)]
def compute():
    counts = [c for _, c in ledger]
    total = round(sum(counts), 4)
    mean = round(sum(counts) / len(counts), 4)
    smallest = min(counts)
    largest = max(counts)
    n = len(counts)
    out = {"total": total, "mean": mean, "smallest": smallest, "largest": largest, "n": n}
    return out
outputs = compute()
result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2 = compute()
result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs2.items()))
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
    print(f"{k}: {v}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")
