Uplift Modelling: Prescribing the Right Nudge JAH-PRAI-000010 · 10

Evidence: established Every historical fact is taken from the cited public sources; the demo is a labeled worked example of the record's own method, not a claim about a historical measurement.

Uplift modelling is prescription with a causal question: not who will buy, but who will buy because of the offer. A customer who would buy anyway should not get the discount, and a customer who will never buy should not get it either; the offer should go to the persuadables, the ones whose decision the offer changes. The method compares the treated and the untreated, using randomized trials, and models the difference, the uplift, rather than the outcome. It matters because most targeting wastes money on people who needed no persuading, and uplift is the correction.

History

The history runs from the randomized trials of the twentieth century, through the A/B testing of the 2000s, when web companies made experimentation routine, to the uplift modelling of the 2010s, when the methods for estimating the causal effect became standard. The practice is now routine in marketing, where the question is always causal: did the campaign cause the sale, or would the sale have happened anyway. The field's lesson is that prediction and prescription are different questions, and a model that predicts well can prescribe badly, because it targets the likely buyers rather than the persuadable ones.

Description

Uplift modelling is prescription with a causal question: not who will buy, but who will buy because of the offer. A customer who would buy anyway should not get the discount, and a customer who will never buy should not get it either; the offer should go to the persuadables, the ones whose decision the offer changes. The method compares the treated and the untreated, using randomized trials, and models the difference, the uplift, rather than the outcome. It matters because most targeting wastes money on people who needed no persuading, and uplift is the correction.

The Problem

What is uplift modelling and A/B testing, prescribing the action that changes the outcome, how does it work in practice, and what do its checkable figures show? This record answers that question with the dated history, the method, a worked demo whose figures are printed twice and hash-checked, and a plain statement of its limits.

The Worked Solution

Step 1: run a randomized trial, treating a random half and leaving the other half untreated. Step 2: model the outcome in both groups, and compute the difference, the uplift, for each person. Step 3: rank the people by uplift, and prescribe the action to the top of the list. Step 4: measure the outcome in a fresh trial, and report the gain over targeting by prediction alone. The demo runs a small worked example of the measurement step: it takes five uplift estimates, 0.12, 0.08, 0.15, 0.05, and 0.1, against measured uplifts of 0.1, 0.09, 0.13, 0.06, and 0.11, and reports the mean absolute error and the root mean square error. The demo is a worked example of the error arithmetic, not a record of a historical campaign. The demo recomputes the checkable part of this solution from the key figures {"mae_worked": 0.014, "rmse_worked": 0.0148, "max_gap_worked": 0.02}.

The Answer

The checkable results are the practice and the dates: randomized trials through the twentieth century, A/B testing routine in the 2000s, and uplift modelling standard in the 2010s. The worked example shows the error arithmetic: the gaps are 0.02, 0.01, 0.02, 0.01, and 0.01, giving a mean absolute error of 0.014 and a root mean square error of 0.0148, with a largest gap of 0.02. The result that matters is the smallness: a mean error of 0.014 on uplifts around 0.1 is a useful estimate, and the prescription it supports, targeting the persuadables, is worth more than the prediction it replaces. The demo prints these checked figures: mae: 0.014, max_err: 0.02, n: 5, rmse: 0.0148.

When It Applies

Uplift modelling applies wherever an action must be targeted and the question is causal: marketing, retention, and any intervention where the cost should go to the persuadables. It applies where the trial is randomized, the estimate is validated on a fresh trial, and the gain over prediction alone is reported. It does not apply where no trial was run, where the sample is too small, or where the targeting would be unfair.

Limits

The limits are the limits of causal inference. Uplift needs a randomized trial, and a trial costs money and time, and the estimate is noisy, because the difference of two outcomes is harder to estimate than either outcome alone. The prescription can also be unfair, if the persuadables are defined by characteristics that should not decide who gets an offer. The honest account reports the trial, the noise, and the gain over prediction alone, and it never presents an uplift estimate as if it were certain.

Math Proof

The mathematics is the mathematics of error measurement. The five gaps are 0.02, 0.01, 0.02, 0.01, and 0.01, which sum to 0.07, so the mean absolute error is 0.07 divided by 5, which is 0.014. The squared gaps are 0.0004, 0.0001, 0.0004, 0.0001, and 0.0001, which sum to 0.0011, so the mean squared error is 0.00022 and the root mean square error is the square root of 0.00022, which is 0.0148. The largest single gap is 0.02. Small errors on a worked example prove the arithmetic, not the campaign; the campaign was proved, or not, by its fresh trial. Formal statement: MAE = (1/n) * sum |pred_i - actual_i|; RMSE = sqrt((1/n) * sum (pred_i - actual_i)^2); max error = max |pred_i - actual_i|. The equation states the forecast-error check the demo performs: it takes the worked example's predicted and actual pairs, averages the absolute errors for the MAE, and takes the square root of the averaged squared errors for the RMSE.

Science Proof

The science behind uplift is the science of causation: a randomized trial separates the effect of the action from the characteristics of the people who receive it, because the treatment is assigned by chance rather than by choice. The model estimates the difference the action makes for each person, which is the causal quantity, and the ranking by that difference is the prescription. The science says the method works when the trial is truly random and the sample is large enough, and fails when the treatment leaks into the control group or the sample is too small to estimate the difference. Scientific relation: A forecast is judged by the gap between what was predicted and what happened; the MAE averages that gap and the RMSE punishes large gaps more heavily.. The demo recomputes the checkable arithmetic of this record from the stated figures, runs the computation twice, and compares the two SHA-256 digests; matching digests show the arithmetic is reproducible, and the method text states every input figure.

Working demo program

import hashlib
pairs = [(0.12, 0.1), (0.08, 0.09), (0.15, 0.13), (0.05, 0.06), (0.1, 0.11)]
def compute():
    errs = [abs(p - a) for p, a in pairs]
    mae = round(sum(errs) / len(errs), 4)
    rmse = round((sum(e * e for e in errs) / len(errs)) ** 0.5, 4)
    max_err = round(max(errs), 4)
    n = len(pairs)
    out = {"mae": mae, "rmse": rmse, "max_err": max_err, "n": n}
    return out
outputs = compute()
result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2 = compute()
result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs2.items()))
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
    print(f"{k}: {v}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

import hashlib
class ProgJAHPRAI000010:
    """Working program for this record; run() recomputes the demo result."""
    def run(self):
        import hashlib
        pairs = [(0.12, 0.1), (0.08, 0.09), (0.15, 0.13), (0.05, 0.06), (0.1, 0.11)]
        def compute():
            errs = [abs(p - a) for p, a in pairs]
            mae = round(sum(errs) / len(errs), 4)
            rmse = round((sum(e * e for e in errs) / len(errs)) ** 0.5, 4)
            max_err = round(max(errs), 4)
            n = len(pairs)
            out = {"mae": mae, "rmse": rmse, "max_err": max_err, "n": n}
            return out
        outputs = compute()
        result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
        h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
        outputs2 = compute()
        result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs2.items()))
        h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
        for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
            print(f"{k}: {v}")
        print(f"Result: {result}")
        print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
        print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
        print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")
        return {"deterministic": h1 == h2, "result": result}
if __name__ == "__main__":
    print(ProgJAHPRAI000010().run())

Multi-Objective Optimization: The Pareto Frontier JAH-PRAI-000011 · 11

Evidence: established Every historical fact is taken from the cited public sources; the demo is a labeled worked example of the record's own method, not a claim about a historical measurement.

Multi-objective optimization is prescription when there is no single best answer: given goals that conflict, cost against quality, speed against safety, find the set of options where no goal can be improved without worsening another. That set is the Pareto frontier, named for Vilfredo Pareto, who described it in 1906, and the decision is then a choice along the frontier, which is a matter of values rather than of mathematics. The method matters because real decisions always have several goals, and a method that pretends there is one will hide the trade-off instead of showing it.

History

The history runs from Pareto's 1906 work on economic efficiency, through the twentieth century, when the frontier became the standard frame for engineering design and policy, to the present, where it is the standard way to present a trade-off. The methods are evolutionary algorithms and scalarization, which find the frontier by searching, and the practice is to show the decision maker the frontier and let them choose. The lesson is that the mathematics can find the frontier, but it cannot choose along it, because the choice is about what matters, and what matters is a human judgment.

Description

Multi-objective optimization is prescription when there is no single best answer: given goals that conflict, cost against quality, speed against safety, find the set of options where no goal can be improved without worsening another. That set is the Pareto frontier, named for Vilfredo Pareto, who described it in 1906, and the decision is then a choice along the frontier, which is a matter of values rather than of mathematics. The method matters because real decisions always have several goals, and a method that pretends there is one will hide the trade-off instead of showing it.

The Problem

What is multi-objective optimization, Pareto efficiency, and the frontier of trade-offs, how does it work in practice, and what do its checkable figures show? This record answers that question with the dated history, the method, a worked demo whose figures are printed twice and hash-checked, and a plain statement of its limits.

The Worked Solution

Step 1: state the objectives and the constraints. Step 2: search for the set of options where no objective can be improved without worsening another, the Pareto frontier. Step 3: present the frontier, with the trade-offs visible. Step 4: let the decision maker choose along the frontier, and record the choice and its reasons. The demo runs a small worked example of the choice step: it takes five frontier options with costs of 50, 70, 90, 110, and 130 and benefits of 100, 130, 155, 175, and 190, and reports the best option by benefit per cost, the total cost, and the total benefit. The demo is a worked example of the choice arithmetic, not a record of a historical design. The demo recomputes the checkable part of this solution from the key figures {"pareto_year": 1906, "best_ratio_worked": 2.0, "total_cost_worked": 450, "total_benefit_worked": 750}.

The Answer

The checkable results are the dates: Pareto described the frontier in 1906, and the methods are standard today. The worked example shows the choice arithmetic: the benefit-per-cost ratios are 2.0, 1.8571, 1.7222, 1.5909, and 1.4615, so the best option by ratio is the first, with a ratio of 2.0, and the totals are a cost of 450 and a benefit of 750. The result that matters is the shape: the ratios fall as the cost rises, which is the signature of diminishing returns, and the frontier shows the trade-off, so the decision maker can see what each extra unit of benefit costs. The demo prints these checked figures: best: option 1, best_ratio: 2.0, total_benefit: 750, total_cost: 450.

When It Applies

Multi-objective optimization applies wherever goals conflict and the trade-off must be shown: engineering design, policy, and any decision with several objectives. It applies where the objectives are stated, the frontier is shown, and the choice is recorded. It does not apply where a single objective is pretended, where an objective is left out, or where the choice along the frontier is made without the decision maker.

Limits

The limits are the limits of the frame. The frontier shows the trade-off only among the objectives that were stated, and an objective left out is a trade-off hidden. The choice along the frontier is a judgment, and a method that pretends to make it has smuggled in a value. The search can also miss parts of the frontier, especially where it is discontinuous. The honest account states all the objectives, shows the whole frontier, and records the choice and its reasons, because the choice is the decision maker's, not the algorithm's.

Math Proof

The mathematics is the mathematics of ratios. The five options have benefit-per-cost ratios of 100 over 50, which is 2.0; 130 over 70, which is 1.8571; 155 over 90, which is 1.7222; 175 over 110, which is 1.5909; and 190 over 130, which is 1.4615. The best ratio is 2.0, for the first option. The total cost is 50 plus 70 plus 90 plus 110 plus 130, which is 450, and the total benefit is 100 plus 130 plus 155 plus 175 plus 190, which is 750. These numbers describe one worked example, and they show the method: compare the ratios, and see the diminishing returns as the cost rises. Formal statement: score = benefit / cost for each option; best = the option with the largest score; total cost = sum of costs; total benefit = sum of benefits. The equation states the choice check the demo performs: it scores every option by benefit divided by cost, picks the largest score as the best option, and totals the costs and benefits.

Science Proof

The science behind the Pareto frontier is the science of trade-offs: when goals conflict, the best that can be done is to find the options that waste nothing, and the frontier is exactly that set. The science says the frontier can be found by search, but the choice along it cannot be made by mathematics, because it depends on the values of the decision maker. The method works where the objectives can be stated and the frontier can be searched, and it fails where an objective is left out, because the frontier will then hide the trade-off it was meant to show. Scientific relation: A prescriptive choice compares options by what each one returns for what it costs; the best option is the one with the largest benefit per unit of cost.. Working demo program

import hashlib
options = [('option 1', 50, 100), ('option 2', 70, 130), ('option 3', 90, 155), ('option 4', 110, 175), ('option 5', 130, 190)]
def compute():
    scored = [(b / c, name) for name, c, b in options]
    best_ratio_raw, best = max(scored)
    best_ratio = round(best_ratio_raw, 4)
    total_cost = sum(c for _, c, _ in options)
    total_benefit = sum(b for _, _, b in options)
    out = {"best": best, "best_ratio": best_ratio, "total_cost": total_cost, "total_benefit": total_benefit}
    return out
outputs = compute()
result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2 = compute()
result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs2.items()))
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
    print(f"{k}: {v}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

Full working program

import hashlib
class ProgJAHPRAI000011:
    """Working program for this record; run() recomputes the demo result."""
    def run(self):
        import hashlib
        options = [('option 1', 50, 100), ('option 2', 70, 130), ('option 3', 90, 155), ('option 4', 110, 175), ('option 5', 130, 190)]
        def compute():
            scored = [(b / c, name) for name, c, b in options]
            best_ratio_raw, best = max(scored)
            best_ratio = round(best_ratio_raw, 4)
            total_cost = sum(c for _, c, _ in options)
            total_benefit = sum(b for _, _, b in options)
            out = {"best": best, "best_ratio": best_ratio, "total_cost": total_cost, "total_benefit": total_benefit}
            return out
        outputs = compute()
        result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
        h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
        outputs2 = compute()
        result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs2.items()))
        h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
        for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
            print(f"{k}: {v}")
        print(f"Result: {result}")
        print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
        print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
        print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")
        return {"deterministic": h1 == h2, "result": result}
if __name__ == "__main__":
    print(ProgJAHPRAI000011().run())

Constraint Programming: Scheduling Under Rules JAH-PRAI-000012 · 12

Evidence: established Every historical fact is taken from the cited public sources; the demo is a labeled worked example of the record's own method, not a claim about a historical measurement.

Constraint programming is prescription by rules that must not be broken: given tasks, resources, and hard constraints, find an assignment that satisfies them all, and then optimize within the feasible set. It grew up in the 1970s and 1980s as a way to solve scheduling and configuration problems, where the constraints are the whole problem and the objective is secondary. It works by propagation: each constraint narrows the possibilities, and the search proceeds by choosing a variable, trying a value, and backtracking when a constraint is violated. It is the standard tool for timetables, rosters, and job-shop scheduling, wherever the rules are hard and the schedule must respect them all.

History

The history runs from the constraint work of the 1970s, through the systems of the 1980s, which made propagation and search practical, to the present, where constraint programming solves timetables, rosters, and factory schedules. The method's power is its handling of hard constraints: a schedule that violates a rule is not a schedule, and the search never wastes time on one. The practice is now standard in airlines, schools, and factories, where the constraints are numerous and the schedule must satisfy them all. The lesson is that the constraints are the problem, and the optimization is the refinement.

Description

Constraint programming is prescription by rules that must not be broken: given tasks, resources, and hard constraints, find an assignment that satisfies them all, and then optimize within the feasible set. It grew up in the 1970s and 1980s as a way to solve scheduling and configuration problems, where the constraints are the whole problem and the objective is secondary. It works by propagation: each constraint narrows the possibilities, and the search proceeds by choosing a variable, trying a value, and backtracking when a constraint is violated. It is the standard tool for timetables, rosters, and job-shop scheduling, wherever the rules are hard and the schedule must respect them all.

The Problem

What is constraint programming for scheduling and allocation under hard rules, how does it work in practice, and what do its checkable figures show? This record answers that question with the dated history, the method, a worked demo whose figures are printed twice and hash-checked, and a plain statement of its limits.

The Worked Solution

Step 1: state the variables, their domains, and the constraints that must hold. Step 2: propagate the constraints, narrowing each variable's domain to the values that can still satisfy them. Step 3: search by choosing a variable and trying a value, backtracking when a constraint is violated. Step 4: report the feasible assignment, and optimize within it if an objective is given. The demo runs a small worked example of the counting step: it takes five scheduling runs with constraints checked of 240, 310, 180, 290, and 260, and reports the total, the mean, the smallest, and the largest. The demo is a worked example of the ledger arithmetic, not a record of a historical schedule. The demo recomputes the checkable part of this solution from the key figures {"total_constraints_worked": 1280, "mean_constraints_worked": 256.0}.

The Answer

The checkable results are the practice and the dates: constraint programming developed in the 1970s and 1980s, and the methods standard today. The worked example shows the counting arithmetic: the five runs check 240, 310, 180, 290, and 260 constraints, for a total of 1280, a mean of 256.0, a smallest of 180, and a largest of 310. The result that matters is the scale: hundreds of constraints checked per run is the normal size of a scheduling problem, and the propagation is what makes that scale tractable. The demo prints these checked figures: largest: 310, mean: 256.0, n: 5, smallest: 180, total: 1280.

When It Applies

Constraint programming applies wherever hard rules must be satisfied and the schedule must respect them all: timetables, rosters, and job-shop scheduling. It applies where the constraints are stated, the propagation is strong, and the feasibility is proved. It does not apply where the constraints are soft, where the search would take too long, or where a constraint left out would make the schedule fail in practice.

Limits

The limits are the limits of hard rules. A problem with no feasible assignment will be proved infeasible, which is correct but not helpful, and a problem with weak constraints will take too long to search. The model must also be complete: a constraint left out will produce a schedule that violates it in practice, and the schedule will fail when it is used. The honest account states all the constraints, proves feasibility or infeasibility, and checks the schedule against the real world before it is used.

Math Proof

The mathematics is the mathematics of a ledger. The five runs check 240, 310, 180, 290, and 260 constraints, which sum to 1280, and 1280 divided by 5 is 256.0. The smallest run checks 180 constraints and the largest checks 310. These numbers describe one worked example, and they make the method concrete: constraint programming is counting and pruning, and the count is the measure of the work. Formal statement: total = sum of the counts; mean = total / n; smallest = min of the counts; largest = max of the counts. The equation states the ledger check the demo performs: it adds the named counts, divides by how many there are for the mean, and picks out the smallest and the largest.

Science Proof

The science behind constraint programming is the science of propagation: a constraint that must hold narrows the possibilities, and the narrowing compounds, so the search space shrinks before the search begins. The science says the method works when the constraints are strong, because strong constraints prune hard, and fails when they are weak, because the search must then explore nearly everything. The backtracking search is complete: it will find a feasible assignment if one exists, and prove that none exists if it does not, which is a guarantee that heuristics cannot give. Scientific relation: A ledger of counts is summarised by its total, its mean, and its extremes; those four numbers are the checkable summary of the record's figures.. Working demo program

import hashlib
ledger = [('run 1', 240), ('run 2', 310), ('run 3', 180), ('run 4', 290), ('run 5', 260)]
def compute():
    counts = [c for _, c in ledger]
    total = round(sum(counts), 4)
    mean = round(sum(counts) / len(counts), 4)
    smallest = min(counts)
    largest = max(counts)
    n = len(counts)
    out = {"total": total, "mean": mean, "smallest": smallest, "largest": largest, "n": n}
    return out
outputs = compute()
result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2 = compute()
result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs2.items()))
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
    print(f"{k}: {v}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

Reinforcement Learning as Prescription: Learning What to Do JAH-PRAI-000013 · 13

Evidence: established Every historical fact is taken from the cited public sources; the demo is a labeled worked example of the record's own method, not a claim about a historical measurement.

Reinforcement learning is prescription learned by doing: an agent tries actions, observes the rewards, and learns a policy that maximizes the total reward over time. The framework is the Markov decision process, and the methods are Q-learning, policy gradients, and the actor-critic family, all of which estimate the value of actions from experience. The field's textbook is Sutton and Barto's Reinforcement Learning: An Introduction, first published in 1998, and the methods have since won games, controlled robots, and managed resources. The prescription is the policy, and the policy is learned, not written.

History

The history runs from the trial-and-error learning of the early twentieth century, through Bellman's 1957 dynamic programming, to the Q-learning of 1989 and the deep reinforcement learning of the 2010s, when networks learned to play games at superhuman level. The 2010s brought the demonstrations: Atari games learned from pixels, Go mastered by AlphaGo in 2016, and robots controlled by learned policies. The practice is now standard wherever a policy can be learned by trial, in simulation or in the world, and the prescription is the learned policy. The lesson is that a policy can be learned where it cannot be written, provided the trying is cheap and the rewards are honest.

Description

Reinforcement learning is prescription learned by doing: an agent tries actions, observes the rewards, and learns a policy that maximizes the total reward over time. The framework is the Markov decision process, and the methods are Q-learning, policy gradients, and the actor-critic family, all of which estimate the value of actions from experience. The field's textbook is Sutton and Barto's Reinforcement Learning: An Introduction, first published in 1998, and the methods have since won games, controlled robots, and managed resources. The prescription is the policy, and the policy is learned, not written.

The Problem

What is reinforcement learning for prescription, from Sutton and Barto to prescriptive policies, how does it work in practice, and what do its checkable figures show? This record answers that question with the dated history, the method, a worked demo whose figures are printed twice and hash-checked, and a plain statement of its limits.

The Worked Solution

Step 1: state the states, the actions, and the rewards, with a discount for the future. Step 2: let the agent try actions, observe the rewards, and update its estimates of each action's value. Step 3: improve the policy toward the actions with the highest estimated value, and keep exploring so the estimates stay honest. Step 4: test the learned policy in fresh episodes, and report its total reward. The demo runs a small worked example of the measurement step: it takes five episode rewards, 120, 150, 135, 165, and 145, against baseline rewards of 100, 110, 105, 115, and 108, and reports the mean absolute gap and the root mean square gap. The demo is a worked example of the comparison arithmetic, not a record of a historical training run. The demo recomputes the checkable part of this solution from the key figures {"bellman_year": 1957, "sutton_barto_year": 1998, "alphago_year": 2016, "mae_gap_worked": 35.4, "rmse_gap_worked": 36.794, "max_gap_worked": 50}.

The Answer

The checkable results are the dates: Bellman 1957, Q-learning 1989, Sutton and Barto 1998, and AlphaGo 2016. The worked example shows the comparison arithmetic: the gaps are 20, 40, 30, 50, and 37, giving a mean absolute gap of 35.4 and a root mean square gap of 36.794, with a largest gap of 50. The result that matters is the improvement: the learned policy beats the baseline on every episode, by twenty to fifty reward units, which is the signature of a policy that has learned something real. The demo prints these checked figures: mae: 35.4, max_err: 50, n: 5, rmse: 36.794.

When It Applies

Reinforcement learning applies wherever a policy can be learned by trial and the trying is cheap: games, robotics in simulation, and resource management. It applies where the rewards are honest, the episodes are numerous, and the learned policy is tested fresh. It does not apply where the trying is expensive, where the rewards can be gamed, or where the simulation differs from the world in ways that matter.

Limits

The limits are the limits of learning by trial. The agent needs many episodes, and each episode costs time or money, so the learning is expensive where the trying is expensive. The rewards must be specified honestly, because an agent will optimize exactly what it is rewarded for, including the loopholes. And a policy learned in simulation may not transfer to the world, because the simulation leaves out what the world contains. The honest account reports the episodes, the rewards, and the transfer test, because a policy that works in simulation and fails in the world is not a policy at all.

Math Proof

The mathematics is the mathematics of error measurement. The five gaps are 20, 40, 30, 50, and 37, which sum to 177, so the mean absolute gap is 177 divided by 5, which is 35.4. The squared gaps are 400, 1600, 900, 2500, and 1369, which sum to 6769, so the mean squared gap is 1353.8 and the root mean square gap is the square root of 1353.8, which is 36.794. The largest single gap is 50. The root mean square gap exceeds the mean absolute gap because the gap of 50 dominates the squares, which is the signature of one episode where the learned policy did much better than the baseline. Formal statement: MAE = (1/n) * sum |pred_i - actual_i|; RMSE = sqrt((1/n) * sum (pred_i - actual_i)^2); max error = max |pred_i - actual_i|. The equation states the forecast-error check the demo performs: it takes the worked example's predicted and actual pairs, averages the absolute errors for the MAE, and takes the square root of the averaged squared errors for the RMSE.

Science Proof

The science behind reinforcement learning is the science of learning from reward: an agent that tries actions and observes rewards can estimate the value of each action, and the estimates improve with experience. The exploration-exploitation trade-off states the tension: the agent must try new actions to learn, and use the best known actions to earn, and the balance decides how fast it learns. The science says the method works when the trying is cheap, the rewards are honest, and the episodes are numerous, and it fails when the trying is expensive, the rewards are misspecified, or the world changes faster than the learning. Scientific relation: A forecast is judged by the gap between what was predicted and what happened; the MAE averages that gap and the RMSE punishes large gaps more heavily.. The demo recomputes the checkable arithmetic of this record from the stated figures, runs the computation twice, and compares the two SHA-256 digests; matching digests show the arithmetic is reproducible, and the method text states every input figure.

Working demo program

import hashlib
pairs = [(120, 100), (150, 110), (135, 105), (165, 115), (145, 108)]
def compute():
    errs = [abs(p - a) for p, a in pairs]
    mae = round(sum(errs) / len(errs), 4)
    rmse = round((sum(e * e for e in errs) / len(errs)) ** 0.5, 4)
    max_err = round(max(errs), 4)
    n = len(pairs)
    out = {"mae": mae, "rmse": rmse, "max_err": max_err, "n": n}
    return out
outputs = compute()
result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2 = compute()
result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs2.items()))
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
    print(f"{k}: {v}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

import hashlib
class ProgJAHPRAI000013:
    """Working program for this record; run() recomputes the demo result."""
    def run(self):
        import hashlib
        pairs = [(120, 100), (150, 110), (135, 105), (165, 115), (145, 108)]
        def compute():
            errs = [abs(p - a) for p, a in pairs]
            mae = round(sum(errs) / len(errs), 4)
            rmse = round((sum(e * e for e in errs) / len(errs)) ** 0.5, 4)

            n = len(pairs)
            out = {"mae": mae, "rmse": rmse, "max_err": max_err, "n": n}
            return out

        h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
        outputs2 = compute()

Inventory Optimization: The Economic Order Quantity JAH-PRAI-000014 · 14

Evidence: established Every historical fact is taken from the cited public sources; the demo is a labeled worked example of the record's own method, not a claim about a historical measurement.

Inventory optimization is prescription for the warehouse: given the demand, the cost of ordering, and the cost of holding stock, prescribe how much to order and when. The economic order quantity, published by Ford Whitman Harris in 1913, gives the answer for steady demand: order the quantity that balances the ordering cost against the holding cost, and the balance is a square root. The newsvendor model handles the uncertain case, a single ordering decision before an uncertain demand, and prescribes the quantity that balances the cost of ordering too much against the cost of ordering too little. Both are still taught and still used, because the trade-off they capture has not changed.

History

The history runs from Harris's 1913 paper, through the twentieth century, when inventory theory became a standard part of operations research, to the present, where the models run inside every large supply chain. The economic order quantity's power is its simplicity: one formula, two costs, and a prescription that can be computed by hand. The newsvendor model's power is its handling of uncertainty: it prescribes a quantity that depends on the demand distribution and on the two costs, and it is the standard frame for perishable goods, from newspapers to fashion. The practice is now standard, and the models are the baseline against which fancier methods are judged.

Description

Inventory optimization is prescription for the warehouse: given the demand, the cost of ordering, and the cost of holding stock, prescribe how much to order and when. The economic order quantity, published by Ford Whitman Harris in 1913, gives the answer for steady demand: order the quantity that balances the ordering cost against the holding cost, and the balance is a square root. The newsvendor model handles the uncertain case, a single ordering decision before an uncertain demand, and prescribes the quantity that balances the cost of ordering too much against the cost of ordering too little. Both are still taught and still used, because the trade-off they capture has not changed.

The Problem

What is inventory optimization, the economic order quantity from Harris 1913, and the newsvendor model, how does it work in practice, and what do its checkable figures show? This record answers that question with the dated history, the method, a worked demo whose figures are printed twice and hash-checked, and a plain statement of its limits.

The Worked Solution

Step 1: state the demand, the ordering cost, and the holding cost. Step 2: compute the economic order quantity as the square root of twice the demand times the ordering cost divided by the holding cost. Step 3: for uncertain demand, compute the newsvendor quantity from the demand distribution and the two costs. Step 4: order the prescribed quantity, and measure the cost when the demand arrives. The demo runs a small worked example of the choice step: it takes five order options with costs of 200, 260, 180, 320, and 240 and benefits of 500, 620, 460, 760, and 580, and reports the best option by benefit per cost, the total cost, and the total benefit. The demo is a worked example of the choice arithmetic, not a record of a historical inventory decision. The demo recomputes the checkable part of this solution from the key figures {"harris_year": 1913, "best_ratio_worked": 2.5556, "total_cost_worked": 1200, "total_benefit_worked": 2920}.

The Answer

The checkable results are the dates: Harris published the economic order quantity in 1913, and the models remain standard today. The worked example shows the choice arithmetic: the benefit-per-cost ratios are 2.5, 2.3846, 2.5556, 2.375, and 2.4167, so the best option is the third, with a ratio of 2.5556, and the totals are a cost of 1200 and a benefit of 2920. The result that matters is the method: the best order is the one that returns the most per unit of cost, and the economic order quantity generalizes that comparison to the trade-off between ordering and holding. The demo prints these checked figures: best: order 3, best_ratio: 2.5556, total_benefit: 2920, total_cost: 1200.

When It Applies

Inventory optimization applies wherever stock must be ordered and the costs can be stated: retail, manufacturing, and any warehouse with steady or uncertain demand. It applies where the demand is measured, the costs are known, and the prescription is re-solved as the demand changes. It does not apply where the demand is unknown, where the costs cannot be stated, or where the prescription would be followed without checking it against the real demand.

Limits

The limits are the limits of the assumptions. The economic order quantity assumes steady demand, known costs, and instant replenishment, and real demand is none of those. The newsvendor model assumes a single ordering decision and a known demand distribution, and real decisions are repeated and the distribution is estimated. The honest account states the assumptions, measures the demand, and re-solves as the demand changes, because an inventory prescription based on last year's demand is a prescription for last year's warehouse.

Math Proof

The mathematics is the mathematics of ratios. The five options have benefit-per-cost ratios of 500 over 200, which is 2.5; 620 over 260, which is 2.3846; 460 over 180, which is 2.5556; 760 over 320, which is 2.375; and 580 over 240, which is 2.4167. The best ratio is 2.5556, for the third option. The total cost is 200 plus 260 plus 180 plus 320 plus 240, which is 1200, and the total benefit is 500 plus 620 plus 460 plus 760 plus 580, which is 2920. These numbers describe one worked example, and they show the method: compare the ratios, pick the best, and report the totals. Formal statement: score = benefit / cost for each option; best = the option with the largest score; total cost = sum of costs; total benefit = sum of benefits. The equation states the choice check the demo performs: it scores every option by benefit divided by cost, picks the largest score as the best option, and totals the costs and benefits.

Science Proof

The science behind inventory optimization is the science of the trade-off: ordering often costs much in ordering and little in holding, ordering rarely costs little in ordering and much in holding, and the optimum balances the two. The square root in the formula is the signature of that balance: doubling the demand increases the optimal order by the square root of two, not by two, because the holding cost grows with the order size. The newsvendor model adds uncertainty: the optimal quantity balances the cost of a unit unsold against the cost of a unit short, and the balance point is a quantile of the demand distribution. The science says the prescription is only as good as the demand estimate, and the estimate is the hard part. Scientific relation: A prescriptive choice compares options by what each one returns for what it costs; the best option is the one with the largest benefit per unit of cost.. The demo recomputes the checkable arithmetic of this record from the stated figures, runs the computation twice, and compares the two SHA-256 digests; matching digests show the arithmetic is reproducible, and the method text states every input figure.

Working demo program

import hashlib
options = [('order 1', 200, 500), ('order 2', 260, 620), ('order 3', 180, 460), ('order 4', 320, 760), ('order 5', 240, 580)]
def compute():
    scored = [(b / c, name) for name, c, b in options]
    best_ratio_raw, best = max(scored)
    best_ratio = round(best_ratio_raw, 4)
    total_cost = sum(c for _, c, _ in options)
    total_benefit = sum(b for _, _, b in options)
    out = {"best": best, "best_ratio": best_ratio, "total_cost": total_cost, "total_benefit": total_benefit}
    return out
outputs = compute()
result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2 = compute()
result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs2.items()))
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
    print(f"{k}: {v}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

Scheduling Optimization: The Job Shop JAH-PRAI-000015 · 15

Evidence: established Every historical fact is taken from the cited public sources; the demo is a labeled worked example of the record's own method, not a claim about a historical measurement.

Scheduling optimization is prescription for time: given jobs that need machines, machines that can do one job at a time, and deadlines to meet, prescribe the order that finishes earliest or costs least. The job-shop problem states it formally, and it is hard in the same sense as routing: no fast exact method exists for large instances, so the practice is heuristics, dispatching rules, and constraint programming. Johnson's rule, published in 1954, solves the two-machine case exactly, by ordering the jobs according to their processing times, and it remains the textbook example of a scheduling rule that can be proved optimal. The systems now schedule factories, hospitals, and airlines, and the savings are counted in time.

History

The history runs from Johnson's 1954 paper on the two-machine flow shop, through the decades of scheduling theory, to the present, where the methods run in every factory and hospital. The field's lesson is that the order matters: the same jobs on the same machines can finish hours apart depending on the order, and the right order is worth finding. The practice is now standard, with dispatching rules for the simple cases, heuristics for the hard ones, and constraint programming where the rules are hard and must all be satisfied. The discipline is the deadline: a schedule is judged by whether it meets the deadlines, not by whether it is elegant.

Description

Scheduling optimization is prescription for time: given jobs that need machines, machines that can do one job at a time, and deadlines to meet, prescribe the order that finishes earliest or costs least. The job-shop problem states it formally, and it is hard in the same sense as routing: no fast exact method exists for large instances, so the practice is heuristics, dispatching rules, and constraint programming. Johnson's rule, published in 1954, solves the two-machine case exactly, by ordering the jobs according to their processing times, and it remains the textbook example of a scheduling rule that can be proved optimal. The systems now schedule factories, hospitals, and airlines, and the savings are counted in time.

The Problem

What is scheduling optimization, job-shop scheduling, and Johnson's rule from 1954, how does it work in practice, and what do its checkable figures show? This record answers that question with the dated history, the method, a worked demo whose figures are printed twice and hash-checked, and a plain statement of its limits.

The Worked Solution

Step 1: state the jobs, their processing times on each machine, and their deadlines. Step 2: choose an order, by Johnson's rule for two machines or by a heuristic for more. Step 3: compute the completion time and check the deadlines. Step 4: report the schedule, the completion time, and the jobs that miss their deadlines. The demo runs a small worked example of the measurement step: it takes five scheduled completion times, 42, 38, 45, 40, and 44 hours, against target times of 40, 40, 40, 40, and 40 hours, and reports the mean absolute error and the root mean square error. The demo is a worked example of the error arithmetic, not a record of a historical schedule. The demo recomputes the checkable part of this solution from the key figures {"johnson_year": 1954, "mae_worked": 2.6, "rmse_worked": 3.1305, "max_gap_worked": 5}.

The Answer

The checkable results are the dates: Johnson's rule published 1954, and scheduling methods standard ever since. The worked example shows the error arithmetic: the gaps are 2, 2, 5, 0, and 4, giving a mean absolute error of 2.6 and a root mean square error of 3.1305, with a largest gap of 5. The result that matters is the interpretation: a mean lateness of 2.6 hours on a forty-hour target is a schedule that mostly meets its deadlines, and the largest gap of five hours is the job to investigate, because it may mark a bottleneck the schedule did not handle. The demo prints these checked figures: mae: 2.6, max_err: 5, n: 5, rmse: 3.1305.

When It Applies

Scheduling optimization applies wherever jobs must be ordered on shared machines and deadlines must be met: factories, hospitals, and airlines. It applies where the processing times are known, the schedule is re-solved as conditions change, and the deadlines are reported honestly. It does not apply where the times are unknown, where the machines are unreliable, or where a schedule would be followed without slack for the unexpected.

Limits

The limits are the limits of planning under uncertainty. Processing times are estimates, machines break, and jobs arrive unexpectedly, so a schedule planned in the morning may be obsolete by the afternoon. The honest account re-solves as conditions change, keeps slack for the unexpected, and never presents a schedule as if the future were certain. A schedule that cannot adapt is a schedule that will fail, and the failure will be blamed on the schedule rather than on the world, which is unfair to both.

Math Proof

The mathematics is the mathematics of error measurement. The five gaps are 2, 2, 5, 0, and 4, which sum to 13, so the mean absolute error is 13 divided by 5, which is 2.6. The squared gaps are 4, 4, 25, 0, and 16, which sum to 49, so the mean squared error is 9.8 and the root mean square error is the square root of 9.8, which is 3.1305. The largest single gap is 5. Small errors on a worked example prove the arithmetic, not the schedule; the schedule was proved, or not, by whether it met its deadlines in practice. Formal statement: MAE = (1/n) * sum |pred_i - actual_i|; RMSE = sqrt((1/n) * sum (pred_i - actual_i)^2); max error = max |pred_i - actual_i|. The equation states the forecast-error check the demo performs: it takes the worked example's predicted and actual pairs, averages the absolute errors for the MAE, and takes the square root of the averaged squared errors for the RMSE.

Science Proof

The science behind scheduling is the science of ordering: the completion time of a set of jobs depends on the order they run, and the right order minimizes the waiting. Johnson's rule proves the point for two machines: order the jobs by their shortest processing time, and the makespan is minimal. The science says the problem is hard in general, because the number of orders grows factorially, and the practical answer is a heuristic that finds a good order quickly. The method works where the processing times are known and the machines are reliable, and it fails where the times are uncertain or the machines break down. Scientific relation: A forecast is judged by the gap between what was predicted and what happened; the MAE averages that gap and the RMSE punishes large gaps more heavily.. Formal equation

MAE = (1/n) * sum |pred_i - actual_i|; RMSE = sqrt((1/n) * sum (pred_i - actual_i)^2); max error = max |pred_i - actual_i|

Formal equation, in plain words

The equation states the forecast-error check the demo performs: it takes the worked example's predicted and actual pairs, averages the absolute errors for the MAE, and takes the square root of the averaged squared errors for the RMSE.

Formal status

stated, not machine-checked

Scientific equation

A forecast is judged by the gap between what was predicted and what happened; the MAE averages that gap and the RMSE punishes large gaps more heavily.

Proof sketch

The demo recomputes the checkable arithmetic of this record from the stated figures, runs the computation twice, and compares the two SHA-256 digests; matching digests show the arithmetic is reproducible, and the method text states every input figure.

Proof status

The arithmetic is machine-checked by the demo; the scientific claim is stated, not machine-checked.

Results reported

The checkable results are the dates: Johnson's rule published 1954, and scheduling methods standard ever since. The worked example shows the error arithmetic: the gaps are 2, 2, 5, 0, and 4, giving a mean absolute error of 2.6 and a root mean square error of 3.1305, with a largest gap of 5. The result that matters is the interpretation: a mean lateness of 2.6 hours on a forty-hour target is a schedule that mostly meets its deadlines, and the largest gap of five hours is the job to investigate, because it may mark a bottleneck the schedule did not handle. The demo prints these checked figures: mae: 2.6, max_err: 5, n: 5, rmse: 3.1305.

Skeptical analysis

The limits are the limits of planning under uncertainty. Processing times are estimates, machines break, and jobs arrive unexpectedly, so a schedule planned in the morning may be obsolete by the afternoon. The honest account re-solves as conditions change, keeps slack for the unexpected, and never presents a schedule as if the future were certain. A schedule that cannot adapt is a schedule that will fail, and the failure will be blamed on the schedule rather than on the world, which is unfair to both.

Working demo program

import hashlib
pairs = [(42, 40), (38, 40), (45, 40), (40, 40), (44, 40)]
def compute():
    errs = [abs(p - a) for p, a in pairs]
    mae = round(sum(errs) / len(errs), 4)
    rmse = round((sum(e * e for e in errs) / len(errs)) ** 0.5, 4)
    max_err = round(max(errs), 4)
    n = len(pairs)
    out = {"mae": mae, "rmse": rmse, "max_err": max_err, "n": n}
    return out
outputs = compute()
result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2 = compute()
result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs2.items()))
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
    print(f"{k}: {v}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

Portfolio Optimization: Markowitz and the Efficient Frontier JAH-PRAI-000016 · 16

Evidence: established Every historical fact is taken from the cited public sources; the demo is a labeled worked example of the record's own method, not a claim about a historical measurement.

Portfolio optimization is prescription for money: given assets with expected returns and risks, prescribe the mix that gives the best return for a chosen level of risk, or the least risk for a chosen return. Harry Markowitz founded the theory in 1952, showing that the risk of a portfolio depends on how its assets move together, not only on how risky each one is alone, and that diversification works because assets that do not move together cancel each other's swings. The efficient frontier is the set of portfolios where no return can be gained without more risk, and the choice along it is the investor's, not the mathematics'.

History

The history runs from Markowitz's 1952 paper, through the 1960s, when the theory became the standard frame for investment, to the present, where it underlies portfolio construction everywhere. The theory's power is its treatment of correlation: two risky assets can combine into a safer portfolio if they do not move together, and the mathematics shows exactly how. The challenges came from estimation: the expected returns and the correlations must be estimated from history, and the estimates are noisy, so the optimized portfolio can be fragile. The modern practice adds constraints and robustness, and the theory remains the standard frame, because it states the trade-off clearly.

Description

Portfolio optimization is prescription for money: given assets with expected returns and risks, prescribe the mix that gives the best return for a chosen level of risk, or the least risk for a chosen return. Harry Markowitz founded the theory in 1952, showing that the risk of a portfolio depends on how its assets move together, not only on how risky each one is alone, and that diversification works because assets that do not move together cancel each other's swings. The efficient frontier is the set of portfolios where no return can be gained without more risk, and the choice along it is the investor's, not the mathematics'.

The Problem

What is portfolio optimization, modern portfolio theory from Markowitz 1952, and the efficient frontier, how does it work in practice, and what do its checkable figures show? This record answers that question with the dated history, the method, a worked demo whose figures are printed twice and hash-checked, and a plain statement of its limits.

The Worked Solution

Step 1: estimate each asset's expected return, risk, and correlation with the others. Step 2: compute the efficient frontier, the portfolios where no return can be gained without more risk. Step 3: choose along the frontier according to the investor's tolerance for risk. Step 4: report the mix, the expected return, and the risk, and rebalance as the estimates change. The demo runs a small worked example of the choice step: it takes five portfolio options with costs of 90, 120, 75, 150, and 100 and benefits of 180, 228, 165, 270, and 200, and reports the best option by benefit per cost, the total cost, and the total benefit. The demo is a worked example of the choice arithmetic, not a record of a historical portfolio. The demo recomputes the checkable part of this solution from the key figures {"markowitz_year": 1952, "best_ratio_worked": 2.2, "total_cost_worked": 535, "total_benefit_worked": 1043}.

The Answer

The checkable results are the dates: Markowitz published the theory in 1952, and it remains the standard frame today. The worked example shows the choice arithmetic: the benefit-per-cost ratios are 2.0, 1.9, 2.2, 1.8, and 2.0, so the best option is the third, with a ratio of 2.2, and the totals are a cost of 535 and a benefit of 1043. The result that matters is the method: the best mix is the one that returns the most per unit of cost, and the frontier shows the trade-off, so the investor can see what each extra unit of return costs in risk. The demo prints these checked figures: best: portfolio 3, best_ratio: 2.2, total_benefit: 1043, total_cost: 535.

When It Applies

Portfolio optimization applies wherever a mix of assets must be chosen and the trade-off must be shown: investment, pension funds, and any allocation where risk and return conflict. It applies where the estimates are stated, the frontier is shown, and the mix is tested out of sample. It does not apply where the estimates are treated as certain, where the constraints are hidden, or where the choice along the frontier is made without the investor.

Limits

The limits are the limits of estimation. The expected returns and correlations are estimated from history, and the estimates are noisy, so the optimized portfolio can be fragile, a problem known as error maximization. The theory also assumes the investor cares only about mean and variance, which is not true of real investors, and it assumes the future resembles the past, which fails in a crisis. The honest account adds constraints, tests the portfolio out of sample, and never presents an optimized mix as if the estimates were certain.

Math Proof

The mathematics is the mathematics of ratios. The five options have benefit-per-cost ratios of 180 over 90, which is 2.0; 228 over 120, which is 1.9; 165 over 75, which is 2.2; 270 over 150, which is 1.8; and 200 over 100, which is 2.0. The best ratio is 2.2, for the third option. The total cost is 90 plus 120 plus 75 plus 150 plus 100, which is 535, and the total benefit is 180 plus 228 plus 165 plus 270 plus 200, which is 1043. These numbers describe one worked example, and they show the method: compare the ratios, pick the best, and report the totals. Formal statement: score = benefit / cost for each option; best = the option with the largest score; total cost = sum of costs; total benefit = sum of benefits. The equation states the choice check the demo performs: it scores every option by benefit divided by cost, picks the largest score as the best option, and totals the costs and benefits.

Science Proof

The science behind portfolio theory is the science of diversification: the risk of a portfolio is the sum of the variances and the covariances, and the covariances can be negative, so assets that move in opposite directions reduce the total risk. The science says the benefit of diversification is real but limited: it removes the risk that is specific to one asset, and it cannot remove the risk that affects all assets at once. The method works where the estimates are stable, and it fails where they are noisy, because the optimizer will exploit the noise and produce a portfolio that is optimal for the estimates and poor for the future. Scientific relation: A prescriptive choice compares options by what each one returns for what it costs; the best option is the one with the largest benefit per unit of cost.. Working demo program

import hashlib
options = [('portfolio 1', 90, 180), ('portfolio 2', 120, 228), ('portfolio 3', 75, 165), ('portfolio 4', 150, 270), ('portfolio 5', 100, 200)]
def compute():
    scored = [(b / c, name) for name, c, b in options]
    best_ratio_raw, best = max(scored)
    best_ratio = round(best_ratio_raw, 4)
    total_cost = sum(c for _, c, _ in options)
    total_benefit = sum(b for _, _, b in options)
    out = {"best": best, "best_ratio": best_ratio, "total_cost": total_cost, "total_benefit": total_benefit}
    return out
outputs = compute()
result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2 = compute()
result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs2.items()))
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
    print(f"{k}: {v}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

Dynamic Pricing: Revenue Management from Airlines JAH-PRAI-000017 · 17

Evidence: established Every historical fact is taken from the cited public sources; the demo is a labeled worked example of the record's own method, not a claim about a historical measurement.

Dynamic pricing is prescription for the price: given the demand, the remaining capacity, and the time left to sell it, prescribe the price that maximizes the revenue. The airlines invented it in the 1980s, as revenue management, when American Airlines built the system that let it sell the same seat at many prices, filling the plane while protecting the revenue from business travellers. The method spread to hotels, rental cars, and e-commerce, and it now prices much of what is sold online. It works because the demand is uncertain and the capacity is fixed, so the price must adapt as the selling window closes.

History

The history runs from the deregulation of American airlines in 1978, through the revenue-management systems of the 1980s, when American Airlines' system became the model, to the present, where dynamic pricing runs in hotels, retail, and ride-hailing. The economics drive it: a seat that flies empty earns nothing, and a seat sold too cheaply leaves money behind, so the price must balance the two. The practice is now standard, and the methods are forecasting plus optimization, predicting the demand at each price and choosing the price that maximizes the expected revenue. The concerns spread with the practice: a price that changes by the minute can feel unfair, and the systems must be judged by their transparency as well as by their revenue.

Description

Dynamic pricing is prescription for the price: given the demand, the remaining capacity, and the time left to sell it, prescribe the price that maximizes the revenue. The airlines invented it in the 1980s, as revenue management, when American Airlines built the system that let it sell the same seat at many prices, filling the plane while protecting the revenue from business travellers. The method spread to hotels, rental cars, and e-commerce, and it now prices much of what is sold online. It works because the demand is uncertain and the capacity is fixed, so the price must adapt as the selling window closes.

The Problem

What is dynamic pricing and revenue management, from airline yield management in the 1980s, how does it work in practice, and what do its checkable figures show? This record answers that question with the dated history, the method, a worked demo whose figures are printed twice and hash-checked, and a plain statement of its limits.

The Worked Solution

Step 1: forecast the demand at each candidate price, from the history and the current bookings. Step 2: compute the expected revenue for each price, as the price times the expected sales. Step 3: choose the price with the highest expected revenue, subject to the capacity and the time left. Step 4: re-solve as bookings arrive, and report the revenue against the fixed-price baseline. The demo runs a small worked example of the choice step: it takes five price options with costs of 30, 40, 25, 50, and 35 and benefits of 120, 150, 105, 180, and 140, and reports the best option by benefit per cost, the total cost, and the total benefit. The demo is a worked example of the choice arithmetic, not a record of a historical pricing decision. The demo recomputes the checkable part of this solution from the key figures {"deregulation_year": 1978, "best_ratio_worked": 4.2, "total_cost_worked": 180, "total_benefit_worked": 695}.

The Answer

The checkable results are the dates: airline deregulation 1978, and revenue management systems in the 1980s. The worked example shows the choice arithmetic: the benefit-per-cost ratios are 4.0, 3.75, 4.2, 3.6, and 4.0, so the best option is the third, with a ratio of 4.2, and the totals are a cost of 180 and a benefit of 695. The result that matters is the method: the best price is the one that returns the most per unit of cost, and the optimization re-solves as the bookings arrive, so the price adapts to the demand. The demo prints these checked figures: best: price 3, best_ratio: 4.2, total_benefit: 695, total_cost: 180.

When It Applies

Dynamic pricing applies wherever capacity is perishable and demand is uncertain: airlines, hotels, rental cars, and e-commerce. It applies where the forecast is honest, the revenue is measured against the baseline, and the pricing can be explained. It does not apply where the price changes would be unfair, where the forecast is unreliable, or where the segmentation cannot be stated.

Limits

The limits are the limits of pricing people. A price that changes by the minute can feel unfair, and a system that charges different people different prices for the same thing must be able to explain why. The forecast can also be wrong, and a price set on a wrong forecast will either leave capacity unsold or sell it too cheaply. The honest account reports the revenue against the fixed-price baseline, states the segmentation, and keeps the pricing explainable, because a price that cannot be explained will be resented.

Math Proof

The mathematics is the mathematics of ratios. The five options have benefit-per-cost ratios of 120 over 30, which is 4.0; 150 over 40, which is 3.75; 105 over 25, which is 4.2; 180 over 50, which is 3.6; and 140 over 35, which is 4.0. The best ratio is 4.2, for the third option. The total cost is 30 plus 40 plus 25 plus 50 plus 35, which is 180, and the total benefit is 120 plus 150 plus 105 plus 180 plus 140, which is 695. These numbers describe one worked example, and they show the method: compare the ratios, pick the best, and report the totals. Formal statement: score = benefit / cost for each option; best = the option with the largest score; total cost = sum of costs; total benefit = sum of benefits. The equation states the choice check the demo performs: it scores every option by benefit divided by cost, picks the largest score as the best option, and totals the costs and benefits.

Science Proof

The science behind dynamic pricing is the science of perishable capacity: a seat, a room, or a car that goes unsold earns nothing, and the price must be set to sell the capacity before it perishes. The science says the optimal price balances the margin against the volume, and the balance shifts as the deadline approaches: early, the price can be high, because there is time to sell; late, it must fall, because the capacity will otherwise perish. The method works where the demand can be forecast and the customers can be segmented, and it fails where the forecast is wrong or the segmentation is unfair. Scientific relation: A prescriptive choice compares options by what each one returns for what it costs; the best option is the one with the largest benefit per unit of cost.. History

The history runs from the deregulation of American airlines in 1978, through the revenue-management systems of the 1980s, when American Airlines' system became the model, to the present, where dynamic pricing runs in hotels, retail, and ride-hailing. The economics drive it: a seat that flies empty earns nothing, and a seat sold too cheaply leaves money behind, so the price must balance the two. The practice is now standard, and the methods are forecasting plus optimization, predicting the demand at each price and choosing the price that maximizes the expected revenue. The concerns spread with the practice: a price that changes by the minute can feel unfair, and the systems must be judged by their transparency as well as by their revenue.

Problem

What is dynamic pricing and revenue management, from airline yield management in the 1980s, how does it work in practice, and what do its checkable figures show? This record answers that question with the dated history, the method, a worked demo whose figures are printed twice and hash-checked, and a plain statement of its limits.

Method

Step 1: forecast the demand at each candidate price, from the history and the current bookings. Step 2: compute the expected revenue for each price, as the price times the expected sales. Step 3: choose the price with the highest expected revenue, subject to the capacity and the time left. Step 4: re-solve as bookings arrive, and report the revenue against the fixed-price baseline. The demo runs a small worked example of the choice step: it takes five price options with costs of 30, 40, 25, 50, and 35 and benefits of 120, 150, 105, 180, and 140, and reports the best option by benefit per cost, the total cost, and the total benefit. The demo is a worked example of the choice arithmetic, not a record of a historical pricing decision.

Dataset

Dataset: dynamic pricing; airline deregulation 1978; revenue management 1980s. Demo: worked example choosing the best of five price options by benefit per cost. The equation states the choice check the demo performs: it scores every option by benefit divided by cost, picks the largest score as the best option, and totals the costs and benefits.

Skeptical analysis

The limits are the limits of pricing people. A price that changes by the minute can feel unfair, and a system that charges different people different prices for the same thing must be able to explain why. The forecast can also be wrong, and a price set on a wrong forecast will either leave capacity unsold or sell it too cheaply. The honest account reports the revenue against the fixed-price baseline, states the segmentation, and keeps the pricing explainable, because a price that cannot be explained will be resented.

Working demo program

import hashlib
options = [('price 1', 30, 120), ('price 2', 40, 150), ('price 3', 25, 105), ('price 4', 50, 180), ('price 5', 35, 140)]
def compute():
    scored = [(b / c, name) for name, c, b in options]
    best_ratio_raw, best = max(scored)
    best_ratio = round(best_ratio_raw, 4)
    total_cost = sum(c for _, c, _ in options)
    total_benefit = sum(b for _, _, b in options)
    out = {"best": best, "best_ratio": best_ratio, "total_cost": total_cost, "total_benefit": total_benefit}
    return out
outputs = compute()
result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2 = compute()
result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs2.items()))
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
    print(f"{k}: {v}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

Human Oversight: The Override and the Loop JAH-PRAI-000018 · 18

Evidence: established Every historical fact is taken from the cited public sources; the demo is a labeled worked example of the record's own method, not a claim about a historical measurement.

Human oversight is the discipline that keeps prescription honest: a system may recommend, but a human decides, and the human must be able to see the reasoning, question it, and override it. The design is called human-in-the-loop, and it matters because the system's prescription is only as good as its model, and the model is always incomplete. The override is the mechanism: the human can reject the prescription, and the rejection is recorded, so the system can be audited and improved. A prescriptive system without an override is a system that has taken the decision from the person who must answer for it, and that is not a design but an abdication.

History

The history runs from the earliest expert systems, which were built as advisers rather than deciders, through the 1980s and 1990s, when the advisory model became the standard for high-stakes systems, to the present, where human-in-the-loop is the required design for medical, legal, and financial prescription. The lesson was learned from the failures: systems that decided without oversight made mistakes that nobody caught, because nobody was watching, and the response was to keep the human in the loop by design, not by habit. The practice is now standard in regulation, which requires a human to be able to review and override automated decisions that affect people.

Description

Human oversight is the discipline that keeps prescription honest: a system may recommend, but a human decides, and the human must be able to see the reasoning, question it, and override it. The design is called human-in-the-loop, and it matters because the system's prescription is only as good as its model, and the model is always incomplete. The override is the mechanism: the human can reject the prescription, and the rejection is recorded, so the system can be audited and improved. A prescriptive system without an override is a system that has taken the decision from the person who must answer for it, and that is not a design but an abdication.

The Problem

What is human oversight of prescriptive AI, human-in-the-loop design, and the override, how does it work in practice, and what do its checkable figures show? This record answers that question with the dated history, the method, a worked demo whose figures are printed twice and hash-checked, and a plain statement of its limits.

The Worked Solution

Step 1: present the prescription with its reasoning, its evidence, and its uncertainty. Step 2: let the human accept, modify, or reject it, and record the decision. Step 3: measure the override rate, and investigate when it is very high or very low. Step 4: use the overrides to improve the system, and report the rate. The demo runs a small worked example of the counting step: it takes five weeks with overrides of 12, 18, 9, 15, and 11 out of 100 prescriptions, and reports the total, the mean, the smallest, and the largest. The demo is a worked example of the ledger arithmetic, not a record of a historical oversight program. The demo recomputes the checkable part of this solution from the key figures {"total_overrides_worked": 65, "mean_overrides_worked": 13.0}.

The Answer

The checkable results are the practice and its documentation: advisory design from the 1970s, and human-in-the-loop the standard for high-stakes prescription today. The worked example shows the counting arithmetic: the five weeks record 12, 18, 9, 15, and 11 overrides, for a total of 65, a mean of 13.0, a smallest of 9, and a largest of 18. The result that matters is the interpretation: an override rate of about thirteen percent is healthy, because it says the human is engaged but not rejecting everything, and a rate near zero would be the warning sign, because it would say the human has stopped checking. The demo prints these checked figures: largest: 18, mean: 13.0, n: 5, smallest: 9, total: 65.

When It Applies

Human oversight applies wherever a prescription affects a person and the decision must be answerable: medicine, law, finance, and any high-stakes prescription. It applies where the reasoning is shown, the override is easy, and the rate is measured. It does not apply as a formality, it does not apply where the human has no time or information to check, and it does not apply where the override is recorded but never read.

Limits

The limits are the limits of human attention. A human who must check a hundred prescriptions a day will stop checking, and a human who is given no time to check cannot check. The override is also only as good as the information the human is given: a prescription without its reasoning cannot be questioned, and a human without the authority to override will not. The honest account measures the override rate, gives the human the time and the information to check, and never treats the loop as a formality.

Math Proof

The mathematics is the mathematics of a ledger. The five weeks record 12, 18, 9, 15, and 11 overrides, which sum to 65, and 65 divided by 5 is 13.0. The smallest week records 9 overrides and the largest records 18. These numbers describe one worked example, and they make the discipline concrete: oversight is counted, and the count is the evidence that the loop is alive. Formal statement: total = sum of the counts; mean = total / n; smallest = min of the counts; largest = max of the counts. The equation states the ledger check the demo performs: it adds the named counts, divides by how many there are for the mean, and picks out the smallest and the largest.

Science Proof

The science behind oversight is the science of automation bias: people tend to accept what a machine says, even when it is wrong, and the tendency grows as the machine is usually right. The design must fight that tendency, by showing the reasoning, by making the override easy, and by measuring the rate. The science says the loop works when the human is engaged, informed, and empowered, and it fails when the human is a rubber stamp, asked to approve what they cannot check in the time they are given. The measure of the loop is the override rate: too low, and the human is not checking; too high, and the system is not helping. Scientific relation: A ledger of counts is summarised by its total, its mean, and its extremes; those four numbers are the checkable summary of the record's figures.. Working demo program

import hashlib
ledger = [('week 1', 12), ('week 2', 18), ('week 3', 9), ('week 4', 15), ('week 5', 11)]
def compute():
    counts = [c for _, c in ledger]
    total = round(sum(counts), 4)
    mean = round(sum(counts) / len(counts), 4)
    smallest = min(counts)
    largest = max(counts)
    n = len(counts)
    out = {"total": total, "mean": mean, "smallest": smallest, "largest": largest, "n": n}
    return out
outputs = compute()
result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2 = compute()
result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs2.items()))
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
    print(f"{k}: {v}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")
