Prescriptive Maintenance: From Prediction to Action JAH-PRAI-000019 · 19

Evidence: established Every historical fact is taken from the cited public sources; the demo is a labeled worked example of the record's own method, not a claim about a historical measurement.

Prescriptive maintenance is the step beyond prediction: not merely saying a machine will fail, but prescribing what to do about it, when to do it, and what parts to have ready. It combines the prediction of remaining life with the optimization of the maintenance schedule, so the repair happens at the cheapest moment, with the right parts, before the failure. It matters because a prediction without a prescription is only half a system: the operator still has to decide, and the decision is where the money is saved or lost. The practice is now standard in factories, power plants, and airlines, where the maintenance is scheduled by the system and checked by the engineer.

History

The history runs from the predictive maintenance of the 2010s, through the prescriptive step, when the optimization was added to the prediction, to the present, where the systems prescribe the action, the timing, and the parts. The economics drive it: a failure caught early is a repair, a failure caught late is a replacement, and the difference is the value of the prescription. The practice is now standard wherever the equipment is expensive and the downtime is costly, and the systems are judged by the failures they prevent and the maintenance they save, not by the accuracy of the prediction alone.

Description

Prescriptive maintenance is the step beyond prediction: not merely saying a machine will fail, but prescribing what to do about it, when to do it, and what parts to have ready. It combines the prediction of remaining life with the optimization of the maintenance schedule, so the repair happens at the cheapest moment, with the right parts, before the failure. It matters because a prediction without a prescription is only half a system: the operator still has to decide, and the decision is where the money is saved or lost. The practice is now standard in factories, power plants, and airlines, where the maintenance is scheduled by the system and checked by the engineer.

The Problem

What is prescriptive maintenance, from predicting failure to prescribing the repair, how does it work in practice, and what do its checkable figures show? This record answers that question with the dated history, the method, a worked demo whose figures are printed twice and hash-checked, and a plain statement of its limits.

The Worked Solution

Step 1: predict the remaining useful life from the sensor data. Step 2: optimize the maintenance schedule, choosing the time and the action that minimize the total cost. Step 3: prescribe the repair, the timing, and the parts, with the reasoning shown. Step 4: measure the outcome, the failures prevented and the maintenance saved, and update the model. The demo runs a small worked example of the choice step: it takes five maintenance options with costs of 500, 700, 400, 900, and 600 and benefits of 1500, 1900, 1250, 2400, and 1750, and reports the best option by benefit per cost, the total cost, and the total benefit. The demo is a worked example of the choice arithmetic, not a record of a historical maintenance program. The demo recomputes the checkable part of this solution from the key figures {"best_ratio_worked": 3.125, "total_cost_worked": 3100, "total_benefit_worked": 8800}.

The Answer

The checkable results are the practice and the dates: predictive maintenance from the 2010s, and prescriptive maintenance the current standard. The worked example shows the choice arithmetic: the benefit-per-cost ratios are 3.0, 2.7143, 3.125, 2.6667, and 2.9167, so the best option is the third, with a ratio of 3.125, and the totals are a cost of 3100 and a benefit of 8800. The result that matters is the method: the best maintenance is the one that returns the most per unit of cost, and the prescription is worth more than the prediction, because it says what to do, not only what will happen. The demo prints these checked figures: best: maintenance 3, best_ratio: 3.125, total_benefit: 8800, total_cost: 3100.

When It Applies

Prescriptive maintenance applies wherever equipment is expensive, downtime is costly, and the prediction is accurate: factories, power plants, and airlines. It applies where the prediction and the optimization are combined, the costs are known, and the outcome is measured. It does not apply where the prediction is unreliable, where the costs cannot be stated, or where the prescription cannot be carried out.

Limits

The limits are the limits of the prediction and the costs. A wrong prediction will prescribe the wrong time, and a wrong cost estimate will prescribe the wrong action. The prescription also needs the parts and the people to be available, and a prescription that cannot be carried out is not a prescription. The honest account reports the failures prevented, the maintenance saved, and the prescriptions that were not followed, because all three are the measure of the system.

Math Proof

The mathematics is the mathematics of ratios. The five options have benefit-per-cost ratios of 1500 over 500, which is 3.0; 1900 over 700, which is 2.7143; 1250 over 400, which is 3.125; 2400 over 900, which is 2.6667; and 1750 over 600, which is 2.9167. The best ratio is 3.125, for the third option. The total cost is 500 plus 700 plus 400 plus 900 plus 600, which is 3100, and the total benefit is 1500 plus 1900 plus 1250 plus 2400 plus 1750, which is 8800. These numbers describe one worked example, and they show the method: compare the ratios, pick the best, and report the totals. Formal statement: score = benefit / cost for each option; best = the option with the largest score; total cost = sum of costs; total benefit = sum of benefits. The equation states the choice check the demo performs: it scores every option by benefit divided by cost, picks the largest score as the best option, and totals the costs and benefits.

Science Proof

The science behind prescriptive maintenance is the science of combining prediction with optimization: the prediction says when the failure will come, and the optimization says when to act, balancing the cost of acting early against the cost of failing late. The science says the combination is worth more than either part alone, because a prediction without a prescription leaves the decision to the operator, and an optimization without a prediction has nothing to optimize. The method works where the prediction is accurate and the costs are known, and it fails where either is wrong. Scientific relation: A prescriptive choice compares options by what each one returns for what it costs; the best option is the one with the largest benefit per unit of cost.. Working demo program

import hashlib
options = [('maintenance 1', 500, 1500), ('maintenance 2', 700, 1900), ('maintenance 3', 400, 1250), ('maintenance 4', 900, 2400), ('maintenance 5', 600, 1750)]
def compute():
    scored = [(b / c, name) for name, c, b in options]
    best_ratio_raw, best = max(scored)
    best_ratio = round(best_ratio_raw, 4)
    total_cost = sum(c for _, c, _ in options)
    total_benefit = sum(b for _, _, b in options)
    out = {"best": best, "best_ratio": best_ratio, "total_cost": total_cost, "total_benefit": total_benefit}
    return out
outputs = compute()
result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2 = compute()
result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs2.items()))
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
    print(f"{k}: {v}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

Game Theory: Prescribing Strategy Against an Opponent JAH-PRAI-000020 · 20

Evidence: established Every historical fact is taken from the cited public sources; the demo is a labeled worked example of the record's own method, not a claim about a historical measurement.

Game theory is prescription against an opponent: given the players, their options, and their payoffs, prescribe the strategy that does best, knowing the opponent is doing the same. John Nash's 1950 equilibrium states the solution: a set of strategies where no player can do better by changing alone, so no one has a reason to move. The theory founded modern economics, and it prescribes in auctions, negotiations, and markets, wherever the outcome depends on what everyone does. It works because it models the opponent as rational, and the prescription is the strategy that is best against a rational opponent.

History

The history runs from von Neumann and Morgenstern's 1944 book, through Nash's 1950 equilibrium, to the present, where game theory designs auctions, markets, and mechanisms. The spectrum auctions of the 1990s, designed by game theorists, raised billions for governments, and the theory's lesson is that the rules of the game decide the outcome as much as the players do. The practice is now standard in economics, and the methods are mechanism design, which prescribes the rules so that rational play produces the desired outcome. The discipline is the equilibrium: a prescription that ignores the opponent's response is not a prescription at all.

Description

Game theory is prescription against an opponent: given the players, their options, and their payoffs, prescribe the strategy that does best, knowing the opponent is doing the same. John Nash's 1950 equilibrium states the solution: a set of strategies where no player can do better by changing alone, so no one has a reason to move. The theory founded modern economics, and it prescribes in auctions, negotiations, and markets, wherever the outcome depends on what everyone does. It works because it models the opponent as rational, and the prescription is the strategy that is best against a rational opponent.

The Problem

What is game theory and prescriptive strategy, from Nash 1950 to modern mechanism design, how does it work in practice, and what do its checkable figures show? This record answers that question with the dated history, the method, a worked demo whose figures are printed twice and hash-checked, and a plain statement of its limits.

The Worked Solution

Step 1: state the players, their options, and their payoffs. Step 2: find the equilibria, the sets of strategies where no player can do better by changing alone. Step 3: choose among the equilibria, by payoff, by fairness, or by the mechanism's design. Step 4: prescribe the strategy, and report the equilibrium and the reasoning. The demo runs a small worked example of the counting step: it takes five games with equilibria found of 2, 1, 3, 2, and 1, and reports the total, the mean, the smallest, and the largest. The demo is a worked example of the ledger arithmetic, not a record of a historical game. The demo recomputes the checkable part of this solution from the key figures {"nash_year": 1950, "total_equilibria_worked": 9, "mean_equilibria_worked": 1.8}.

The Answer

The checkable results are the dates: von Neumann and Morgenstern 1944, Nash 1950, and the spectrum auctions of the 1990s. The worked example shows the counting arithmetic: the five games yield 2, 1, 3, 2, and 1 equilibria, for a total of 9, a mean of 1.8, a smallest of 1, and a largest of 3. The result that matters is the interpretation: most games have one or two equilibria, and the prescription must say which one is intended, because the players will coordinate on one of them, and the wrong one is a failure of the prescription. The demo prints these checked figures: largest: 3, mean: 1.8, n: 5, smallest: 1, total: 9.

When It Applies

Game theory applies wherever the outcome depends on what everyone does and the players can be modeled as rational: auctions, negotiations, and markets. It applies where the payoffs are stated, the equilibria are found, and the prescription is tested against real play. It does not apply where the players are not rational, where the payoffs cannot be stated, or where the game modeled is not the game being played.

Limits

The limits are the limits of rationality. Real players are not fully rational, payoffs are hard to specify, and the game that is modeled is rarely the game that is played. A prescription that assumes rationality will fail against an irrational opponent, and a mechanism designed for rational players can be gamed by those who are not. The honest account states the payoffs, says where they came from, and tests the prescription against real play, because a theory that works on paper and fails in the room is not a theory worth following.

Math Proof

The mathematics is the mathematics of a ledger. The five games yield 2, 1, 3, 2, and 1 equilibria, which sum to 9, and 9 divided by 5 is 1.8. The smallest game yields 1 equilibrium and the largest yields 3. These numbers describe one worked example, and they make the point concrete: equilibria are counted, and the count matters, because a game with several equilibria needs a prescription that says which one to play. Formal statement: total = sum of the counts; mean = total / n; smallest = min of the counts; largest = max of the counts. The equation states the ledger check the demo performs: it adds the named counts, divides by how many there are for the mean, and picks out the smallest and the largest.

Science Proof

The science behind game theory is the science of strategic reasoning: a player's best action depends on what the others do, so the solution must be a fixed point, a set of strategies that are best responses to each other. Nash's equilibrium is that fixed point, and the theory shows it exists under general conditions. The science says the prescription must account for the opponent's rationality, and it fails where the opponent is not rational, where the payoffs are misspecified, or where the game is not the one being played. The mechanism-design lesson is that the rules can be chosen, and the right rules make the rational play the desired play. Scientific relation: A ledger of counts is summarised by its total, its mean, and its extremes; those four numbers are the checkable summary of the record's figures.. The demo recomputes the checkable arithmetic of this record from the stated figures, runs the computation twice, and compares the two SHA-256 digests; matching digests show the arithmetic is reproducible, and the method text states every input figure.

Working demo program

import hashlib
ledger = [('game 1', 2), ('game 2', 1), ('game 3', 3), ('game 4', 2), ('game 5', 1)]
def compute():
    counts = [c for _, c in ledger]
    total = round(sum(counts), 4)
    mean = round(sum(counts) / len(counts), 4)
    smallest = min(counts)
    largest = max(counts)
    n = len(counts)
    out = {"total": total, "mean": mean, "smallest": smallest, "largest": largest, "n": n}
    return out
outputs = compute()
result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2 = compute()
result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs2.items()))
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
    print(f"{k}: {v}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

Robust Optimization: Prescribing Under Uncertainty JAH-PRAI-000021 · 21

Evidence: established Every historical fact is taken from the cited public sources; the demo is a labeled worked example of the record's own method, not a claim about a historical measurement.

Robust optimization is prescription for the case where the numbers are not known: given an uncertain parameter, prescribe the action that does best in the worst case, rather than the action that does best on average. The methods were developed by Aharon Ben-Tal and Arkadi Nemirovski in the 1990s, and they answer the question that ordinary optimization cannot: what if the estimate is wrong. The prescription is conservative by design, because it protects against the worst case, and the cost of the protection is the price of robustness. It matters wherever the uncertainty is large and the failure is expensive, which is most of the places that matter.

History

The history runs from the early robust methods of the 1970s, through Ben-Tal and Nemirovski's work in the 1990s, which made robust optimization practical by showing that the robust counterpart of a problem could be solved efficiently, to the present, where the methods are standard in finance, energy, and logistics. The practice is now routine: state the uncertainty set, the range of values the parameter might take, solve for the action that does best in the worst case, and report the price of robustness, the loss against the action that would be best if the parameter were known. The lesson is that the average case is not the case to plan for, when the worst case is expensive.

Description

Robust optimization is prescription for the case where the numbers are not known: given an uncertain parameter, prescribe the action that does best in the worst case, rather than the action that does best on average. The methods were developed by Aharon Ben-Tal and Arkadi Nemirovski in the 1990s, and they answer the question that ordinary optimization cannot: what if the estimate is wrong. The prescription is conservative by design, because it protects against the worst case, and the cost of the protection is the price of robustness. It matters wherever the uncertainty is large and the failure is expensive, which is most of the places that matter.

The Problem

What is robust optimization under uncertainty, from Ben-Tal and Nemirovski in the 1990s, how does it work in practice, and what do its checkable figures show? This record answers that question with the dated history, the method, a worked demo whose figures are printed twice and hash-checked, and a plain statement of its limits.

The Worked Solution

Step 1: state the decision, the objective, and the uncertainty set, the range of values the uncertain parameter might take. Step 2: solve for the action that maximizes the objective in the worst case within the set. Step 3: compute the price of robustness, the loss against the action that would be best if the parameter were known. Step 4: report the robust action, the worst case, and the price, and let the decision maker judge whether the protection is worth it. The demo runs a small worked example of the choice step: it takes five robust options with costs of 80, 100, 65, 120, and 90 and benefits of 200, 240, 175, 280, and 220, and reports the best option by benefit per cost, the total cost, and the total benefit. The demo is a worked example of the choice arithmetic, not a record of a historical robust optimization. The demo recomputes the checkable part of this solution from the key figures {"best_ratio_worked": 2.6923, "total_cost_worked": 455, "total_benefit_worked": 1115}.

The Answer

The checkable results are the dates: Ben-Tal and Nemirovski's work in the 1990s, and robust optimization standard today. The worked example shows the choice arithmetic: the benefit-per-cost ratios are 2.5, 2.4, 2.6923, 2.3333, and 2.4444, so the best option is the third, with a ratio of 2.6923, and the totals are a cost of 455 and a benefit of 1115. The result that matters is the method: the robust choice is the one that does best in the worst case, and the ratio comparison is the transparent way to show it. The demo prints these checked figures: best: robust 3, best_ratio: 2.6923, total_benefit: 1115, total_cost: 455.

When It Applies

Robust optimization applies wherever the uncertainty is large and the failure is expensive: finance, energy, logistics, and any decision where the worst case must be protected against. It applies where the uncertainty set is stated, the price of robustness is reported, and the decision maker judges the trade-off. It does not apply where the uncertainty cannot be bounded, where the price of robustness is hidden, or where the worst case is so unlikely that protecting against it costs more than the failure would.

Limits

The limits are the limits of conservatism. A robust prescription can be so cautious that it forgoes most of the value, and the price of robustness must be stated and judged. The uncertainty set is also a judgment, and a set that is too wide or too narrow will produce a prescription that is either useless or unprotected. The honest account reports the price of robustness, states the uncertainty set, and lets the decision maker judge whether the protection is worth the price.

Math Proof

The mathematics is the mathematics of ratios. The five options have benefit-per-cost ratios of 200 over 80, which is 2.5; 240 over 100, which is 2.4; 175 over 65, which is 2.6923; 280 over 120, which is 2.3333; and 220 over 90, which is 2.4444. The best ratio is 2.6923, for the third option. The total cost is 80 plus 100 plus 65 plus 120 plus 90, which is 455, and the total benefit is 200 plus 240 plus 175 plus 280 plus 220, which is 1115. These numbers describe one worked example, and they show the method: compare the ratios, pick the best, and report the totals. Formal statement: score = benefit / cost for each option; best = the option with the largest score; total cost = sum of costs; total benefit = sum of benefits. The equation states the choice check the demo performs: it scores every option by benefit divided by cost, picks the largest score as the best option, and totals the costs and benefits.

Science Proof

The science behind robust optimization is the science of the worst case: an action that is best on average can be disastrous in the worst case, and a decision maker who cannot afford the disaster should optimize the worst case instead. The science says the uncertainty set is the key judgment: too wide, and the prescription is so conservative that it is useless; too narrow, and it protects against nothing. The method works where the uncertainty can be bounded and the worst case can be stated, and it fails where the uncertainty is unbounded, because the worst case is then infinitely bad. Scientific relation: A prescriptive choice compares options by what each one returns for what it costs; the best option is the one with the largest benefit per unit of cost.. The demo recomputes the checkable arithmetic of this record from the stated figures, runs the computation twice, and compares the two SHA-256 digests; matching digests show the arithmetic is reproducible, and the method text states every input figure.

Working demo program

import hashlib
options = [('robust 1', 80, 200), ('robust 2', 100, 240), ('robust 3', 65, 175), ('robust 4', 120, 280), ('robust 5', 90, 220)]
def compute():
    scored = [(b / c, name) for name, c, b in options]
    best_ratio_raw, best = max(scored)
    best_ratio = round(best_ratio_raw, 4)
    total_cost = sum(c for _, c, _ in options)
    total_benefit = sum(b for _, _, b in options)
    out = {"best": best, "best_ratio": best_ratio, "total_cost": total_cost, "total_benefit": total_benefit}
    return out
outputs = compute()
result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2 = compute()
result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs2.items()))
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
    print(f"{k}: {v}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

import hashlib
class ProgJAHPRAI000021:
    """Working program for this record; run() recomputes the demo result."""
    def run(self):
        import hashlib
        options = [('robust 1', 80, 200), ('robust 2', 100, 240), ('robust 3', 65, 175), ('robust 4', 120, 280), ('robust 5', 90, 220)]
        def compute():
            scored = [(b / c, name) for name, c, b in options]
            best_ratio_raw, best = max(scored)
            best_ratio = round(best_ratio_raw, 4)
            total_cost = sum(c for _, c, _ in options)
            total_benefit = sum(b for _, _, b in options)
            out = {"best": best, "best_ratio": best_ratio, "total_cost": total_cost, "total_benefit": total_benefit}
            return out
        outputs = compute()
        result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
        h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
        outputs2 = compute()
        result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs2.items()))
        h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
        for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
            print(f"{k}: {v}")
        print(f"Result: {result}")
        print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
        print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
        print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")
        return {"deterministic": h1 == h2, "result": result}
if __name__ == "__main__":
    print(ProgJAHPRAI000021().run())

Precision Agriculture: Prescribing for the Field JAH-PRAI-000022 · 22

Evidence: established Every historical fact is taken from the cited public sources; the demo is a labeled worked example of the record's own method, not a claim about a historical measurement.

Precision agriculture is prescription for the field: given the soil, the weather, and the crop, prescribe how much seed, fertilizer, and water each part of the field needs, rather than treating the whole field the same. The methods are sensing, mapping, and variable-rate application, and the data comes from satellites, drones, and soil sensors. The practice spread in the 1990s and 2000s, as GPS made it possible to know where the tractor was and the sensors made it possible to know what the soil needed. It matters because the inputs are expensive and the environment pays for the excess, so prescribing the right amount in the right place saves money and reduces the runoff.

History

The history runs from the early yield mapping of the 1990s, through the GPS-guided tractors of the 2000s, to the present, where variable-rate application is standard on large farms. The economics drive it: fertilizer and seed are expensive, and applying the same rate everywhere wastes the inputs where the soil is rich and starves the crop where it is poor. The practice is now routine on large farms, and the methods are prescription maps, built from soil data and yield history, which the machinery follows automatically. The lesson is that the field is not uniform, and the prescription must not be either.

Description

Precision agriculture is prescription for the field: given the soil, the weather, and the crop, prescribe how much seed, fertilizer, and water each part of the field needs, rather than treating the whole field the same. The methods are sensing, mapping, and variable-rate application, and the data comes from satellites, drones, and soil sensors. The practice spread in the 1990s and 2000s, as GPS made it possible to know where the tractor was and the sensors made it possible to know what the soil needed. It matters because the inputs are expensive and the environment pays for the excess, so prescribing the right amount in the right place saves money and reduces the runoff.

The Problem

What is prescriptive analytics in agriculture, precision farming, and variable-rate application, how does it work in practice, and what do its checkable figures show? This record answers that question with the dated history, the method, a worked demo whose figures are printed twice and hash-checked, and a plain statement of its limits.

The Worked Solution

Step 1: map the field, with soil data, yield history, and sensor readings. Step 2: build a prescription map, stating the rate for each part of the field. Step 3: apply the map with variable-rate machinery, and record what was applied where. Step 4: measure the yield and the input use, and update the map for the next season. The demo runs a small worked example of the measurement step: it takes five field zones with prescribed fertilizer rates of 120, 90, 150, 80, and 110 kilograms per hectare against applied rates of 118, 92, 148, 82, and 108, and reports the mean absolute error and the root mean square error. The demo is a worked example of the error arithmetic, not a record of a historical harvest. The demo recomputes the checkable part of this solution from the key figures {"mae_worked": 2.0, "rmse_worked": 2.0}.

The Answer

The checkable results are the practice and the dates: yield mapping in the 1990s, GPS-guided application in the 2000s, and variable-rate standard today. The worked example shows the error arithmetic: the gaps are 2, 2, 2, 2, and 2, giving a mean absolute error of 2.0 and a root mean square error of 2.0, with a largest gap of 2. The result that matters is the precision: a constant error of two kilograms per hectare on rates around a hundred is a good application, and the value of the prescription is in the variation across zones, not in the average. The demo prints these checked figures: mae: 2.0, max_err: 2, n: 5, rmse: 2.0.

When It Applies

Precision agriculture applies wherever a field varies and the inputs are expensive: large farms with variable soil, where the mapping is accurate and the machinery can vary the rate. It applies where the saving is measured against the uniform baseline, and only where the mapping is good enough to trust. It does not apply where the field is uniform, where the data is thin, or where the cost of the sensing exceeds the saving.

Limits

The limits are the limits of the map. The prescription is only as good as the soil data and the yield history, and a map built on thin data will prescribe noise. The machinery must also be able to vary the rate accurately, and the cost of the sensing and the machinery must be less than the saving. The honest account measures the yield and the input use against the uniform baseline, and reports the saving, because a prescription that costs more than it saves is not a prescription worth following.

Math Proof

The mathematics is the mathematics of error measurement. The five gaps are 2, 2, 2, 2, and 2, which sum to 10, so the mean absolute error is 10 divided by 5, which is 2.0. The squared gaps are 4, 4, 4, 4, and 4, which sum to 20, so the mean squared error is 4.0 and the root mean square error is the square root of 4.0, which is 2.0. The largest single gap is 2. These are deliberately simple numbers, chosen so the arithmetic is transparent: a constant error of two units is a bias, and a bias can be corrected by recalibrating the applicator. Formal statement: MAE = (1/n) * sum |pred_i - actual_i|; RMSE = sqrt((1/n) * sum (pred_i - actual_i)^2); max error = max |pred_i - actual_i|. The equation states the forecast-error check the demo performs: it takes the worked example's predicted and actual pairs, averages the absolute errors for the MAE, and takes the square root of the averaged squared errors for the RMSE.

Science Proof

The science behind precision agriculture is the science of spatial variation: a field's soil varies from place to place, and the crop's needs vary with it, so a uniform rate is wrong everywhere. The sensing and mapping make the variation visible, and the variable-rate machinery makes the prescription possible. The science says the method works where the variation is real and the mapping is accurate, and it fails where the variation is noise, because prescribing noise is worse than prescribing the average. The measurement is the test: the yield and the input use must be compared with the uniform baseline. Scientific relation: A forecast is judged by the gap between what was predicted and what happened; the MAE averages that gap and the RMSE punishes large gaps more heavily.. The demo recomputes the checkable arithmetic of this record from the stated figures, runs the computation twice, and compares the two SHA-256 digests; matching digests show the arithmetic is reproducible, and the method text states every input figure.

Working demo program

import hashlib
pairs = [(120, 118), (90, 92), (150, 148), (80, 82), (110, 108)]
def compute():
    errs = [abs(p - a) for p, a in pairs]
    mae = round(sum(errs) / len(errs), 4)
    rmse = round((sum(e * e for e in errs) / len(errs)) ** 0.5, 4)
    max_err = round(max(errs), 4)
    n = len(pairs)
    out = {"mae": mae, "rmse": rmse, "max_err": max_err, "n": n}
    return out
outputs = compute()
result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2 = compute()
result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs2.items()))
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
    print(f"{k}: {v}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

        pairs = [(120, 118), (90, 92), (150, 148), (80, 82), (110, 108)]
        def compute():
            errs = [abs(p - a) for p, a in pairs]
            mae = round(sum(errs) / len(errs), 4)
            rmse = round((sum(e * e for e in errs) / len(errs)) ** 0.5, 4)
            max_err = round(max(errs), 4)
            n = len(pairs)
            out = {"mae": mae, "rmse": rmse, "max_err": max_err, "n": n}
            return out
        outputs = compute()
        result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
        h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
        outputs2 = compute()
        result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs2.items()))
        h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
        for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
            print(f"{k}: {v}")
        print(f"Result: {result}")
        print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
        print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
        print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")
        return {"deterministic": h1 == h2, "result": result}
if __name__ == "__main__":
    print(ProgJAHPRAI000022().run())

Manufacturing Prescription: Process Optimization JAH-PRAI-000023 · 23

Evidence: established Every historical fact is taken from the cited public sources; the demo is a labeled worked example of the record's own method, not a claim about a historical measurement.

Manufacturing prescription is optimization for the factory floor: given the process, its settings, and its measurements, prescribe the settings that produce the best quality at the lowest cost. The methods are statistical process control, from Walter Shewhart's work in the 1920s, design of experiments, from Ronald Fisher's work in the 1920s and 1930s, and the optimization that followed. The practice is now standard in every large factory, where the process is monitored continuously and the settings are adjusted as the data arrives. It matters because the process drifts, the raw material varies, and the prescription must adapt, or the quality will drift with it.

History

The history runs from Shewhart's control charts in the 1920s, through Fisher's design of experiments in the 1920s and 1930s, to the post-war quality movement and the present, where process optimization runs continuously in factories. The control chart's power is its simplicity: plot the measurement, draw the limits, and act when the point falls outside them. The design of experiments' power is its efficiency: vary several settings at once, and learn the effects with far fewer runs than varying one at a time. The practice is now standard, and the methods are the backbone of manufacturing quality, because they turn the process from an art into a measured, adjustable system.

Description

Manufacturing prescription is optimization for the factory floor: given the process, its settings, and its measurements, prescribe the settings that produce the best quality at the lowest cost. The methods are statistical process control, from Walter Shewhart's work in the 1920s, design of experiments, from Ronald Fisher's work in the 1920s and 1930s, and the optimization that followed. The practice is now standard in every large factory, where the process is monitored continuously and the settings are adjusted as the data arrives. It matters because the process drifts, the raw material varies, and the prescription must adapt, or the quality will drift with it.

The Problem

What is prescriptive analytics in manufacturing, process optimization, and statistical process control, how does it work in practice, and what do its checkable figures show? This record answers that question with the dated history, the method, a worked demo whose figures are printed twice and hash-checked, and a plain statement of its limits.

The Worked Solution

Step 1: measure the process, with its settings and its quality outcomes. Step 2: chart the measurements, and watch for drift beyond the control limits. Step 3: design experiments to find the settings that improve the quality, and prescribe them. Step 4: monitor the new settings, and re-prescribe as the process drifts. The demo runs a small worked example of the measurement step: it takes five process settings with predicted quality scores of 0.92, 0.88, 0.95, 0.85, and 0.9 against measured scores of 0.9, 0.9, 0.93, 0.87, and 0.89, and reports the mean absolute error and the root mean square error. The demo is a worked example of the error arithmetic, not a record of a historical production run. The demo recomputes the checkable part of this solution from the key figures {"shewhart_decade": 1920, "mae_worked": 0.018, "rmse_worked": 0.0184, "max_gap_worked": 0.02}.

The Answer

The checkable results are the dates: Shewhart's control charts in the 1920s, Fisher's design of experiments in the 1920s and 1930s, and the methods standard today. The worked example shows the error arithmetic: the gaps are 0.02, 0.02, 0.02, 0.02, and 0.01, giving a mean absolute error of 0.018 and a root mean square error of 0.0184, with a largest gap of 0.02. The result that matters is the smallness: a mean error of 0.018 on quality scores around 0.9 is a process that is well understood, and the prescription it supports is worth following. The demo prints these checked figures: mae: 0.018, max_err: 0.02, n: 5, rmse: 0.0184.

When It Applies

Manufacturing prescription applies wherever a process is measured and its settings can be adjusted: factories, chemical plants, and any production where quality depends on settings. It applies where the measurements are reliable, the process is monitored, and the prescription is re-solved as the process drifts. It does not apply where the process is not measured, where the measurements are noisy, or where a setting would be followed after the process has moved on.

Limits

The limits are the limits of measurement. A process that is not measured cannot be optimized, and a measurement that is noisy will mislead the optimization. The settings that are optimal today may not be optimal tomorrow, because the raw material varies and the equipment wears. The honest account monitors continuously, re-prescribes as the process drifts, and never presents a setting as if it were permanent.

Math Proof

The mathematics is the mathematics of error measurement. The five gaps are 0.02, 0.02, 0.02, 0.02, and 0.01, which sum to 0.09, so the mean absolute error is 0.09 divided by 5, which is 0.018. The squared gaps are 0.0004, 0.0004, 0.0004, 0.0004, and 0.0001, which sum to 0.0017, so the mean squared error is 0.00034 and the root mean square error is the square root of 0.00034, which is 0.0184. The largest single gap is 0.02. Small errors on a worked example prove the arithmetic, not the process; the process was proved, or not, by its control charts. Formal statement: MAE = (1/n) * sum |pred_i - actual_i|; RMSE = sqrt((1/n) * sum (pred_i - actual_i)^2); max error = max |pred_i - actual_i|. The equation states the forecast-error check the demo performs: it takes the worked example's predicted and actual pairs, averages the absolute errors for the MAE, and takes the square root of the averaged squared errors for the RMSE.

Science Proof

The science behind process optimization is the science of variation: every process varies, and the variation has causes, some common and some special. The control chart separates them: common-cause variation stays inside the limits, and special-cause variation falls outside them and must be investigated. The design of experiments finds the settings that matter, by varying them systematically and measuring the effects. The science says the method works where the measurements are reliable and the process is stable enough to be studied, and it fails where the measurements are noisy or the process changes faster than it can be studied. Scientific relation: A forecast is judged by the gap between what was predicted and what happened; the MAE averages that gap and the RMSE punishes large gaps more heavily.. The demo recomputes the checkable arithmetic of this record from the stated figures, runs the computation twice, and compares the two SHA-256 digests; matching digests show the arithmetic is reproducible, and the method text states every input figure.

Working demo program

import hashlib
pairs = [(0.92, 0.9), (0.88, 0.9), (0.95, 0.93), (0.85, 0.87), (0.9, 0.89)]
def compute():
    errs = [abs(p - a) for p, a in pairs]
    mae = round(sum(errs) / len(errs), 4)
    rmse = round((sum(e * e for e in errs) / len(errs)) ** 0.5, 4)
    max_err = round(max(errs), 4)
    n = len(pairs)
    out = {"mae": mae, "rmse": rmse, "max_err": max_err, "n": n}
    return out
outputs = compute()
result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2 = compute()
result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs2.items()))
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
    print(f"{k}: {v}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

The Limits of Prescriptive AI: Model Error, Ethics, and Accountability JAH-PRAI-000024 · 24

Evidence: established Every historical fact is taken from the cited public sources; the demo is a labeled worked example of the record's own method, not a claim about a historical measurement.

Prescriptive AI has limits that prediction does not, because a prescription is an instruction to act, and an instruction that is wrong harms someone. The first limit is model error: the prescription is only as good as the model, and the model is always incomplete. The second is ethics: a prescription that optimizes one thing may harm another, and the optimization cannot see the harm it does not measure. The third is accountability: when a prescription is followed and something goes wrong, someone must answer for it, and the system cannot. These limits are not reasons to abandon prescription but reasons to prescribe carefully, with the model stated, the ethics examined, and the accountability kept with a human.

History

The history of the limits is the history of the failures: the 2008 crisis showed what happens when an optimization is followed without questioning its model, the COMPAS debate of 2016 showed what happens when a prescription reproduces a bias, and the automation accidents of the 2010s showed what happens when a human stops checking. The field's response has been to require the model to be stated, the prescription to be explainable, and the human to remain accountable. The practice is now standard in regulation, which requires automated decisions that affect people to be reviewable and overridable. The lesson is that the prescription is a recommendation, not a command, and the human who follows it is the one who answers for it.

Description

Prescriptive AI has limits that prediction does not, because a prescription is an instruction to act, and an instruction that is wrong harms someone. The first limit is model error: the prescription is only as good as the model, and the model is always incomplete. The second is ethics: a prescription that optimizes one thing may harm another, and the optimization cannot see the harm it does not measure. The third is accountability: when a prescription is followed and something goes wrong, someone must answer for it, and the system cannot. These limits are not reasons to abandon prescription but reasons to prescribe carefully, with the model stated, the ethics examined, and the accountability kept with a human.

The Problem

What is the limits of prescriptive AI, model error, ethics, and accountability for the prescription, how does it work in practice, and what do its checkable figures show? This record answers that question with the dated history, the method, a worked demo whose figures are printed twice and hash-checked, and a plain statement of its limits.

The Worked Solution

Step 1: state the model, its assumptions, and its limits, before the prescription is used. Step 2: examine the prescription for its effects on people, including the effects the objective does not measure. Step 3: keep a human accountable, with the authority and the information to override. Step 4: audit the prescriptions, and report the errors and the overrides. The demo runs a small worked example of the counting step: it takes five audits with prescriptions reviewed of 40, 55, 35, 60, and 45, and reports the total, the mean, the smallest, and the largest. The demo is a worked example of the ledger arithmetic, not a record of a historical audit. The demo recomputes the checkable part of this solution from the key figures {"crisis_year": 2008, "compas_year": 2016, "total_reviewed_worked": 235, "mean_reviewed_worked": 47.0}.

The Answer

The checkable results are the dates: the 2008 crisis, the COMPAS debate of 2016, and the regulation of automated decisions in the 2010s and 2020s. The worked example shows the counting arithmetic: the five audits review 40, 55, 35, 60, and 45 prescriptions, for a total of 235, a mean of 47.0, a smallest of 35, and a largest of 60. The result that matters is the discipline: prescriptions are audited, the audit is counted, and the count is the evidence that the accountability is real rather than claimed. The demo prints these checked figures: largest: 60, mean: 47.0, n: 5, smallest: 35, total: 235.

When It Applies

The limits apply as a discipline to every prescriptive system: state the model, examine the ethics, keep the human accountable, and audit the prescriptions. They apply to finance, medicine, and any prescription that affects people. They do not apply as a reason to abandon prescription, because a prescription that is honest about its limits is still useful, and they do not apply as an excuse to deploy a system whose model, ethics, and accountability were never examined.

Limits

The limits are the whole subject of this record, so they are stated plainly. Prescriptive AI is limited by its model, by the ethics of what it optimizes, and by the accountability of the human who follows it. It cannot see what its model leaves out, it cannot judge the harms it does not measure, and it cannot answer for a decision. The honest account states the model, examines the ethics, keeps the human accountable, and audits the prescriptions, because a prescription without those is not a prescription worth following.

Math Proof

The mathematics is the mathematics of a ledger. The five audits review 40, 55, 35, 60, and 45 prescriptions, which sum to 235, and 235 divided by 5 is 47.0. The smallest audit reviews 35 prescriptions and the largest reviews 60. These numbers describe one worked example, and they make the discipline concrete: accountability is counted, and the count is the measure of whether it is happening. Formal statement: total = sum of the counts; mean = total / n; smallest = min of the counts; largest = max of the counts. The equation states the ledger check the demo performs: it adds the named counts, divides by how many there are for the mean, and picks out the smallest and the largest.

Science Proof

The science behind the limits is the science of the model's incompleteness: a model leaves things out, and the things left out are often the ones that matter most to the people affected. The ethics is the recognition that optimization is not neutral: it optimizes what it is told to optimize, and the things it is not told to optimize are the things it will sacrifice. The accountability is the recognition that a system cannot answer for a decision, so a human must, and the human must have the information and the authority to do so. The science says the prescription must be treated as advice, and the advice must be checked. Scientific relation: A ledger of counts is summarised by its total, its mean, and its extremes; those four numbers are the checkable summary of the record's figures..   • A1: One record: one system, one method, one worked demo.
  • A2: The full pair of 24 records in this edition, covering the subject end to end.
  • A3: The boundless generator space: up to 1,000,000,000 scales per file, generated on demand.

Working demo program

import hashlib
ledger = [('audit 1', 40), ('audit 2', 55), ('audit 3', 35), ('audit 4', 60), ('audit 5', 45)]
def compute():
    counts = [c for _, c in ledger]
    total = round(sum(counts), 4)
    mean = round(sum(counts) / len(counts), 4)
    smallest = min(counts)
    largest = max(counts)
    n = len(counts)
    out = {"total": total, "mean": mean, "smallest": smallest, "largest": largest, "n": n}
    return out
outputs = compute()
result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2 = compute()
result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs2.items()))
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
    print(f"{k}: {v}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

Full working program

import hashlib
class ProgJAHPRAI000024:
    """Working program for this record; run() recomputes the demo result."""
    def run(self):
        import hashlib
        ledger = [('audit 1', 40), ('audit 2', 55), ('audit 3', 35), ('audit 4', 60), ('audit 5', 45)]
        def compute():
            counts = [c for _, c in ledger]
            total = round(sum(counts), 4)
            mean = round(sum(counts) / len(counts), 4)
            smallest = min(counts)
            largest = max(counts)
            n = len(counts)
            out = {"total": total, "mean": mean, "smallest": smallest, "largest": largest, "n": n}
            return out
        outputs = compute()
        result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
        h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
        outputs2 = compute()
        result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs2.items()))
        h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
        for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
            print(f"{k}: {v}")
        print(f"Result: {result}")
        print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
        print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
        print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")
        return {"deterministic": h1 == h2, "result": result}
if __name__ == "__main__":
    print(ProgJAHPRAI000024().run())
